Bithop Barlow, of North 'Queensland, is one of tr. e few Anglican prelates who do not boast of a university degree. A section of the Australian episcopate was stupid enough to unsuccessfully oppose his promotion because he had not gone through the orthodox academic mill. The Arch- bishop ef Canterbury quietly snubbed these right rev, objectors by conferring on Bishop Barlow the rare distinction of a Lambeth degree. The latest news about Dr. Barlow is eminently to his credit. A cyclone wrecked the Roman Catholic church in Town.,ville, a leading centre of North Queensland, and Bishop Barlow immediately telegraphed offer- ing the use of the local Church of England to the Catholics until they could make other arrange- ments to meet the emergency. That was a happy, fraternal inspiration that in all probability would not have occurred to exceedingly learned episcopal M.A.'s and D.D.'s from Oxford or Cambridge. The Catholics thanked the Anglican bishop for his kind offer, but intimated that they had already secured the use of a public ball for their services.

OLIVER CROMWELL was a staunch Puritan, and could not brook the iast approach ta Popery. "What are these?" he once inquired, as he saw a dozen statues in the niches of a chapel.—" The twelve Apostles," replied the trembling dean.— "Take them down," said Cromwell, "and coin them into money, so that, like their Master, they may 'go about doing good.'" NATURAL AFF/NITY, —An outside passenger by coach had his hat blown over a bridge, and carried away by the stream. "Is it not very singular," said he to a gentleman who was seated beside him, "that my bat took that direction ?"—" No ta replied the latter; "it is natural that a. beaver .ahould take to the water." MATRIMONIAL MODE -OF PROVING INNOCENCE.— Young Bniffkins married Betty Blotchett for her money, but cannot touch it until she dies; and he treats her very badly on account of what he calls her "unjustifiable longevity." The other day Mrs. Sniffkins finding herself ill, sent for a doctor, and declared her belief that she was "poisoned," and that he (Snitikins) "had done it!" "I didn't do it! shouted Sniffkins. "It's all gammon, she isn't poisoned. Prove it, doctor; open her upon the spot—l'm willing. AN EX ANIFTZ OF rr.—" It distresses me," said the distinguished English lecturer—" it actually distresses me that the Americans are such fervent worshippors of the dollar." " I wonder if that is the reason so many of them pay two or three dollars a head to hear you lecture for an hour and a-half ?" suggested a man who had listened to the remark.

Sir George Baden Powell, who is to be married to-day (Saturday), has for sometime been an object of interest to the fair sex, who were unable to un- derstand why he and his brothArs preferred to live together as bachelors in the "ne house in St. George's-place instead of reqdring help from them. The member for the Kirkdale division is fair, not tall, with keen eyes, clean shaven face, a sharp manner, and an active brain. He has many friends on both sides of the house, and presents have flowed in from all quarters. He was the son of an eminent Oxford professor who figured in the " Essays and Reviere and was himself a Balliol man. Since then he has travelled widely and has done work in Victoria, the West Indies, South Africa, Malta, and British Columbia--his last exploits being connected with the settlement of the Behring Sea and seal dis- putes. He got his knighthood for his share in ar- ranging the Constitution of Malta, and will, no doubt, gain further distinction.

PREAMBLE.—An old gentleman, wishing to be at ease on horsebaek, took his horse to a riding master to be taught to amble. Two or three trials were made upon the animal with partial success.— "Come, sir, do you call this an amble ?" said the owner.—" No, sir, replied the equestrian.; "I call ha pre-ambe. A NOSE AND A FlST.—There was a nose—the largest nose this world did ever see; there was a face that ca:Tied it aloft triumphantly. There was a fist that smote that nose, and caused it sore to bleed; there was a man that bore that fist, and gloried in the deed. There was a catchpoll seized that man, a catehpoll lank and wizen ; there was a judge that seutenced him and sent him off to prison. There was a nose all bruised and black „and seamed with many sears ; there was a rueful-looking face behind the prison bars. TEACHING, A FOREIGNER TO SPEAR ENGLISH.— My friend the foreigner called on me to bid me fare- well before he quitted town, and on his departure he said—•• I an, going at the country." I ventured to correct his phraseology by saying that we were accustomed to say "going 11:ito the .country." He thanked rue for this correction, said he had pro- fited by my lcsson, and added, "I will knock into your door on my return." PRECIOUS METAL AND PRECIOUS Mss.—Owing to the attractions of the justrian digging, the shepherds of the whole country are deserting their flocks, and it is to be feared that the consequence will be the destruction of the sheep. Abundance of gold itself will thus be the cause of distress; and unless something is done to remedy this state of things, we shall soon experience a peculiar and unpleasant ill ustra, ion of the present "Great cry and little wool."—Puneh. THE Fcr REBUKED.-111-natured witticisms are often returned to these who make use of them. A French lady of lash ion, who was famous at court for her bon inet, was attacked by a young fop of high rank, with this question, "Pray, madam, was not that fine gown given you by a gallant?"—" No, my good fellow,' was her answer, "you think you are talking to your mother." TIIE H slon Times says an acquaintance of the editor a few weeks since, purohased a box of cigars warranted "first quality." After smoking half -a- dozen he became convinced that they were com- posed of seine other material than tobacco. Taking a small quantity of rich earth he filled a flower- pot, and planted one, and then patiently awaited, fOr a few days, the result. Half-a-dozen buds aypeared, and, at the end of two weeks, as fine a head of cabbage was grown as was ever seen. WELL MANAGED. -A well-known manager, whose name need not be given, was put to his wit's end by the receipt of a letter from his principal tenor, to the effect that he would not sing that evening unless the previous week's salary were paid instanter. The exchequer was empty. How- ever, about five in the evening, a messenger brought post haste, to the singer, a note, enclosing a cheque for £2O. It was after banking hours, and Just in time to save the evening performance. The singer, restored to good humour, appeared and went through the performance as usual; and pre- senting the cheque at the banker's, next morning found—no effects.

The grand old man of the British Nluseura Read- ing Room is unquestionably Mr. John Povey, who is 83 years of age, and has been a reader at the museum for over 60 years. Mr. Povey is a wonder- ful specimen of a hale and happy octogenarian,and throws off "pars " and articles, with a still facile pen. At the age of 21 '..kl r..Povey was a teacher at the training school for the then National Sociey at Westminster, and has devoted the greater portion of his lengthy life to public teaching and private tuition, having himself received his preliminary education at what was known as Tenison's Gram- mar School, tr generally called the Literary School, in Castle-st., Leicester-square. It need hardly be said that Mr. Povey is full of interesting reminis- ces, having been brought into contract with many of the celebrities of age, As a linguist Mr. Povey has undertaken many translations, and lately did the libretto of an English opera into German and Trench, and it was not until a few years ago that he made journalism his principal avocation. Mr. Povey has seen three or four generations of librari- ans at the Museum, and was on intimate terms with Mr. Panizzi, the originator of the great cata- logue.

Madame Sarah Bernhardt was powerfully impressed in Melbourne with the genius of a young Australian sculptor. Mr. E. B. MacKennal, whom she induced to transfer himself and his talent to the northern hemisphere and set up a studio in Paris, That he has not forgotten his native land at the Antipodes he has demonstrated by sending from Paris to Melbourne a striking design for a monu- ment to be erected by the associated trades of that city as a permanent memorial of the eight hours day. It is a group of three figures, the central one a half-draped figure of a woman holding aloft a tablet on which is inscribed the motto, " Eight Hours Labor," the attitude being startling expres- sive of energy and strength. On the left is a pathetic, attenuated figure of a young man typical of the condition of labor under the old harsh condi- tions. But although worn down by fatigue and long hours, he steadfastly grasps the banner of his cause and fixes his eyes on the inspiring motto on the tablet. On the other side is the figure of a stal- wart blacksmith, representative of triumphant labor, grasping with both hands a heavy sledge- hammer. The design has received much commenda- tion in Melbourne. being instinct with poetry, sympathy, and vitality.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, APRIL 8, 1893.

LADIES' LETTER MY DEAREST NELLIE, Again referring to the much debated question of the width of the skirts, one thing I can promies that the old-fashioned style of crinoline can never return ; but that our skirts will be outspreading at the foot there is no doubt, and if this is done in moderation, the effect is exceedingly pretty, though we are so firmly wedded to our long, close skirts that they will die hard with many of us. Quite the simplest way to manage the skirt of to- day is to run stiff muslin into he seams, and the seams only. This holds them out so slightly as to I be almost imperceptible, though the difference can be seen at once if the skirt so treated is placed beside one without this important alteration. It would appear that almost all the hats this year are composed of two different makes and colours of straw, such as brown-and-green chip and I ndtneg, cream-and-brown fine and rough straw, I while the popular trimmings are rosettes of I all sorts and kinds. These little ornaments can be made of piece silk, satin, or velvet, or of ribbon. A long strip of ribbon or material is folded in two, and then pleated into shape, and the rosette is accomplished. Iso often wonder why more girls do not make their own hats and their mothers' bonnets at home, it is such a saving of money, and if a girl is neat-handed, and observant as to how her friend's headgear is put together, she will soon learn to turn out realiy smart and artistic milli- nery. However, my advice is if you cannot make your own bats never go to a second•rate shop for them. Nothing spoils the effect of a nice dress so much as to crown it with a vulgar hat. No, have fewer hats, and buy them at a good shop.

A becoming and very dainty bonnet is of black " nutmeg grater " straw, having a tiny coronet of cowslip blossoms under the brim, and resting on the hair. The sole trimming is one of the new straw bows, shot in delicate shades of yellow, black, and green, with a small bunch of cowslips nestling beside it. The strings are of shot velvet combining the three shades. With regard to hats there appears to be far less novelty in their shape than in their trimming. They are either of enor- mous dimensions or else very tiny. In my opinion we are almost going back to the pork-pie hat of the sixties I Indeed, the long-banished toque is again well to the fore. A charmingly pretty toque in a combination of black and white is of plain felt, trimmed with two twisted bands of black velvet. A small black velvet rosette and three upstanding ostrich feathers, with a cut jet star, are placed rather far back at the side of the crown and snake a very modish head-gear. The very loose blouse is going out of favour, and this season will see the majority of them made over fitted and boned linings. I think this will be a great improvement, for even a well-cut blouse has a way of rucking up at the ides, which makes the trinimest of figures looks untAy. Any jackets that are worn will fit well and have full skirts of three.qu Liter leogth in order to harmonise with the bell skirts. They look well on tall, slight figures.

A very pretty costume is the one designed to take the place in our affections this year that the Eton held last. Its chief recommendation to many will be, I am sure, the ease with which our dis- carded Etons can be altered into its likeness. At the same time, it is eminently smart, comfortable, and up to date. To start with, cut the back of the Eton jacket to an inch below the waist-line, keep- ing its last year's point still intact. The fronts and pointed revers must be slightly shortened, and squared, and the turn-down collar removed, a high one being made to take its place. Great care must be taken in joining to the bodice to sew the new collar exactly over the lines of the old one. This little coat is almost similar in shape to an officer's mess jacket, and must be ornamented with Hussar braiding and frogs.

A very pretty ornamental watch pocket can be made with two pieces of cardboard, one-and-a- half inches in diameter to form the foundation. Over one side of each circle cover w:th cream satin or silk, and upon the reverue or loner side cover with satin padded with wadding; neatly over- hand the two covers on each circle. Upon the front of one circle paint an owl and the significant words, "I'll ,k-zp =n-atch," Now take a piece of Bilk of the same or contrasting colour, three inches long and three fourths of an inch wide, for a puff between the circles. Hem each end, gather, sew to the edge of each circle, leaving one and three- quartet inches for an opening at the top. Take another piece of same S,Z3 as the puff, on one edge run a ribbon (Tom Thumb width) and gather and sew on the back circle for a frill. Sew on suspension ribbons at the back at the ends of frill. The dimensions given are for a small watch, and If designed for a gentlemam's watch must be aa large again. Yours sincerely, JENNIE.

There is such a diversity of lights at Windsor Castle that those with the best eyesight are dazzled by them ; in fact, the chief passage or corridor is illuminated by four different kinds of lights, old and new, and Prince Albert's room is l!ghted up all night. The Queen's eye- sight is very good, she never wears glasses except when reading or writing.

Over 6,000 women in the United States act as post- masters. The largest number in one State, 463, is in Pennsylvania, and 460 in Virginia. There are 256 in 0hi0,243 in New York, 216 in Georgia, 210 in Texas,and 209 in Kentucky. Alaska has only one. Linen Polish. A good polish for linen cuffs is made as follows : Three ounces of white wax, three drachms of spermae cal, half a pound of borax, one and one-half °uric- of gum tragacanth. Melt together, and put a piece the

Let it stand in a warm place (shake till the gums are dissolved, then strain through muslin. Yorkshire Cakes. Cakes made thus are very nice :—Put two ounces of butter and a pint of milk into a clean saucepan, and stand the saucepan by the side of the fire until the but- ter is melted and the milk is luke-warm. Mix two pounds of flour, six teaspoonsful of baking powder, and half a teaspoonful of salt, well together, and then add the milk, beating the whole into a stiff dough, and divide it into cakes. Let the cakes remain for a short time before the fire on a greased tin, and then put them into a slow oven for half an hour.

GIFTS TO WARRINGTON MUSEUM. _- PROPOSED GALLERY OF "WARRINGTON WORTHIES." The Museum, Sze., Committee reported to the meeting of the Warrington Town Council on Tuesday that they I had received the following gifts durinr, the past month : —Northern stone crab (2 specimens') from Mr. John Porter ; black-headed gull, from Mr. L. Bolton ; moths, 64 specimens, from Mr. F. Womersley, per the Field ,Club ; insects, 149 specimens, from Mr. Greening ;golden plover, from Mr. J. Collins, Jun. ; squirrel, from Mr. G. G. White ; four engravings, framed, viz. :—Portraits of P. Greenall, M.P., C. Ilindley, M.P., John Bright, M.P., and Dr. Dalton, from Mr. R. Pierpoint, M.P. ; two drawings, " Wargrave " and " Bewsey," by the Donor, from Mr. P. Beesley ; " Letters of James Smetham " ; Catalogue of Bronzes in the South Kensington Museum ; Landscape in Art, by Gilbert, from Mr. William Davies; a deed of parchment, viz., Letter of Marque against the Duch to William McFarlane, of Liverpool, 1808, from Mrs. Beamont ; " Bewsey," a poem by Fitchett, 1796, from Miss Robinson ; " Essays on song-writing," by Aikin, Warrington, 1774, from Mr. F. Booth ; "Rules of Daresbury Farmers' Club," 1844, from Mr. C. T. Whitley. In moving the adoption of the Committee's minutes, Councillor H. Roberts said he had also pleasure in drawing the attention of the Council to the number of gifts which the minutes recorded. He did not care to select any particular donor for invidious mention but he wished to say that their present member Mr. Pierpoint, had been a generous donor from time to time, and the engravings now referred to they hoped to have added to, and ultimately to form a special gallery of portraits of old Warrington worthies. They were particularly in- debted to Mr. Pierpoint for his gifts one and all of which would be of value in that respect. (Hear, hear.)

CHAPTER XXIII. • home sh , THE MYNERS. ter 1" Northumberland-terrace, Queensstreet, Notting- But hill, was scarcely so ambitious as its name would worth lead one to believe. It consisted of a row of dingy brick dwellings, set back at a distance of a few feet from the highway, a strip of green grass run- ning along its front. Northumberland-terrace was inhabited by struggl- ing professional men, a half-pay officer or two, and the like. No. 80, the destination of Gwen, was occupied by Mr. Myner, the curate, who had become the• husband of Miss Granger, Gwen's former gov- erness. Mr. Myner's health had grown infirm within the last year or two. He had waited many years to acqure an income sufficient to justify him in taking a wife, but scarcely had he married when that hardly won stipend had' failed him. Some one with more money and influence than he had desired to obtain the curacy in which he had become settled, and money and influence had effected his dismissal, and the instalment of their possessor in his s,ead. No other perferment offered itself to him. He had friends, however, and by their advice he had established a family school for boys under the age of twelve years, the number of pupils being limited to twenty. The income derived from this source was not - excessive, while expenses were many and heavy. Masters in various branches must be secured, ser- vants must be hired, fuel and lights must be paid for, rent was high, and boys, however small, must be fed. And so, at the year's end, when the Rev. Mr. Myner and his wife sat down to compare the outgo with the income, they found that they had not a hundred pounds beyond to secure them against that "rainy day," the dread of which haunts so many struggling lives. Gwen knew of the little school, but Mrs. Myner had never given expression in her letters to any anxieties, and the girl could not guess how many troubles her old governess was enduring. But Gwen was not coming to be a burd'en upon the energies of her friends, but rather to seek counsel and assistance in the task of earning her own living. The cab drew up at the kerbstone of No. 80, and the cabman descended, ran up the walk, and pulled the bell. By the time the door opened and a neat housemaid in a spotless white cap appeared upon the threshold, Gwen had placed her fare in the cab- man's hands, and had alighted. She hurried through the mist over the wet and slippery stones, shining now in the light that streamed from the hallway, and stood upon the wide stone step. "Does Mrs. 'dynes live here ?" she inquired. The housemaid answered in the affirmative. Gwen beckoned the cabman to bring in her trunts, the housemaid staring open-mouthed at the unex- pected intrusion. "Will you tell Mrs. Myner that an old friend wishes to see her?" said Gwen, with that sweet and gentle courtesy which distinguished her. "Yes, miss. Step into the reception room, and I will call her," replied the maid. She ushered the visitor into a room at her right, and hurried on her errand. The coachman deposited the trunk in the hall with a bang and departed, slamming the door behind him. The room into which Gwen had been shown was drawing and reception room in one. Its windows fronted the grass plot in front of the house. It was plainly furnished, but with due regard for comfort. Upon either side of the chimney were low, wide bookcases, well filled with books. A few engrav- ings of scriptural subjects adorned the white walls. A wide chintz-covered lounge, chintz-covered easy chairs, and long chintz curtains, all of a deep red colour, and all spotlessly neat, gave a cozy aspect to the apartment. The coziness was increased by the bright glow of the well-kept fire and its reflec- tion upon the well-swept fender. No doubts of her reception entered 'Gwen's mind. She knew that her governess had really and tenderly loved her, and she believed that this house would prove to her a very haven of refuge. Nor was she doomed to disappointment. The rustling of garments was heard in the hall way, and Mrs. Myner entered the room. She was a very tall, very thin lady, with a gentle face and gentle manners. She had a sad and sub- dued expression, as if her cares weighed heavily upon her. But she was cheerful also. Her courage never failed, even when her husband grew dis- couraged. Patient, brave, and hopeful, she was a "well-spring of pleasure" in the house, a true wife, and a mother to the boys under her charge. Gwen arose at her approach. The girl did not speak, waiting for recognition. Three years had wrought considerable change in our young heroine. Mrs. Myner surveyed the slight, elegant figure, the graceful head, with its massive ripples of bronze-coloured hair, the straight Greek features, the sad yet smiling mouth, in doubt and bewilderment. But when her gaze met the full glance of those purple-dark eyes, now with great lights glowing in their sombre depths, Mrs. Myner knew her visitor. " Gwen!' she ejaculated, in amazement and de- light. "Little Gwen!" She opened her arms and the girl flew into them, and was clasped in a fond and tender embrace. "How you have grown !" exclaimed the lady, pushing Gwen away to look at her, and then embracing her again. "Three years have made a great change in you. You have grown into a young lady, and a very lovely one, too, my dear. But where is your escort? who came with you?' "I came alone," answered Gweh, when she had been released, and the two sat down together upon the little chintz-covered lounge. "I have no home, Mrs. Myner, and no friend in all the world but you." Mrs. Myner looked anxious. "Why, what has happened, my dear 7" she asked. "Are the Quillets dead? Or has the squire returned from his travels?" "The Quillets are well," anwered Gwen, "and the squire is still abroad. But Lonemoor has ceased to be a home for me. I have been turned from its shelter." "My dear, I cannot understand." "I never knew until tecently that I was not a relative of Squire Markham," said Gwen. " I was brought up in his house as if I had been his daughter or niece. I had a governess, handsome rooms, and was served by the 'Quillets as if I were their mistress. I was sent to a Paris peitsionnat of the very highest class. How could I suspect that I was the creature of their bounty How could I suspect " "Then you know all?" exclaimed Mrs. Myner. "Do you know I" "Yes, my dear. I know all there is to know of your history." • • "You know that I am the child of a wandering, insane woman, syl?co perished on the moor in a storm, and lies buried in Penistone churchyard ?" "Yes, my dear. I have seen your mother's grave. You visited it with me in childhood, never dream- ing whose grave it was. Do you remember that lonely mound in the far corner of the churchyard, with the broken slab at its head--'' "With the name 'Magdalen' upon it ? " cried Gwen her recollection presenting the picture to her in all its details. "Was that my mother's grave?" Mrs. Myner replied by a gentle caress that meant assent. The hot blood rushed to Gwen's cheeks. "Who rut that name upon the stone?" she asked. "Who dared mark her grave with that name? " "The Quillets, my dear; the same people who cared for you, and gave you all the advantages you have enjoyed." Gwen was silenced. Mrs. Myner gently removed the girl's hat, and drew her nearer to her as she said— "My dear child, you must have known the truth sooner or later. I always dreaded the hour when you should learn it. There was some mystery about your mother which I have never been able to pene- trate. When I went to Lonemoor as your governess, in response to the advertisement of Mr. Quillet, I soon perceived that something was wrong. The smnaprekrihoarm, endolnesudpaypoasewdayrdoudraaprepleadtibvye oaf butler and the housekeeper treated you as their Squire servant made me aware that there was a mystery about you. I went to the Quillets at once and demanded an explanation. They told me what they seem now to have told you." "And you stayed on and cared for me and loved me," cried Gwen. " That revelation made no difference in your treatment of me ! I must have been blind and deaf. I never suspected anything wrong." s cy over dt in truth words •G" 171 re you discovered telling passionate epolied the history of her life since leaving school. She told V the persecution of the Orkneys, of the harshness of the Quillets—yet speaking of the latter with BA gratitude—and of that final scene which had result- ed in her expulsion from Lonemoor. Perhaps it was too soon for deeper confidences. At any rate she made no allusion to the lover she had won and lost, and whom she had so lately seen.

child I Poor thild l" said Mrs. Myner, You did right to come to me, little Gwen. two babies of my own upstairs bright little joys, aild you shall be a third child to me. My home shall be yours. 1 havp wished for a daugu- ter 1" But the good lady sighed as she uttered these %fords.: She loved Gwen, btight, impulsive, warm- hearted Gwen; but another mouth to feed and another form to clothe, 'would make their small tia savings still smaller. alit would be harder than ever to make both ends theet. She could scarcely afford herself the luxury and the delight of having this beautiful high-bred girl an inmate of her home. Gwen read her thoughts. ' "I will be a daughter to you, dear ,Mrs. Myner," she exclaimed, " but,you must let me work lust as you would let your own daughter, you know. If . you can make me of use in your school, let me stay with you. If you have • no'need of me, will you help me to a situation as governess? I want work. I will not sit down in idleness." "We will talk all this over more at our leisure, my dear. You must be tired after your journey. How inconsiderate I am to plunge into a conversa- tion while you are tired and hungry. Did you come from Yorkshire to-day, and alone ?" "I must learn to take care of myself," she said, brightly. "I began this morning. It is singular that the Quillets allowed you to come alone. Their action in doing so does not accord their previous care of you, my dear," inquired Mrs. Myner suddenly, "do you think it possible that the Quillets had any suspicion of your mother's actual identity ?" Gwen started.

"I think it impossible," she said. "They told me she uever uttered a coherent speech while at Lonemoor, and there was no mark upon her cloth- ing, and nothing whatever about her that could lead to any clue to her real name and position. Only her manners and appearance proclaimed her a lady." " I am greatly puzzled," remarked Mrs. Myner. "I could never understand why the Quillets did so much for you. They never loved you, dear; that you know. Why, then, did they educate you? Why, then, did they serve you so respectfully, as if they felt you their superior? Could it be that they invested their money as in a speculation, expecting to receive it again with interest Could it be that, believing your mother a lady, they fancied that some day some rich relation would step forward and claim you and reimburse them 1" "It is possible." - . .

"Yet I know theynever advertised for the friends of your unfortunate ntother. They never tried to discover your relatives. It is all a mystery to me, GWen, and the conduct of the Quillets is as mys- terious as the rest: and how they dat ad make so free with the squire's property amazes me. Ishould think they would have feared his return and his anger. The squire was childless, I believe." "11e had one daughter—Miss Constance Mark- ham," said poor Gwen innocently; "but she died in Berlin eighteen years ago—a year before I was born. She was very beautiful. It was her death that changed the squire and made him a wanderer." "If is all very strange, dear, but we won't puzzle our heals over it longer. You shall belong to us henceforth little Gwen. I haven't fine roo us and a maid to offer you, dear; bat we have warm hearts and a snug home, and you shall have a place in both. Now let me take you upstairs. You shall have a cup of tea, andithen make the acquaintance of Mr. Myner."

he conducted her guest up a narrow stair to a small bedroom upon the second floor. It was a pretty little chamber, with a low tent bedstead, with white draperies and with toilet appurtenances. "This is our ghest chamber, dear," explained the hostess, lighting a candle which stood in a tall candlestick on a shelf. "Now make yourself com- fortable while I go to tell my husband who is here. I will come for you." With a kiss Mrs. Myner withdrew. Gwen bathed her face and hands, brushed her rippling bronze hair, and replaced her oollar and cults with fresh ones, her hand-bag containing all toilet necessaries.

By the time she was dressed, two stout serving women appeared with her trunk. A little later one of them reappeared with a small tray, covered with a white napkin, upon which were spread a dish of toasted muffins, a mutton chop, and a tiny pot of tea. Gwen did justice to this refreshment, having eaten nothing all day. When she had finished, Mrs. Myner appeared, finding her strengthened by her repast, and looking more cheerfully than before. "We will go down now to Mr. Myner," said the curate's wife. "He is in the schoolroom busy with the copy books. The pupils are all in bed, and now comes our happy hour, although it is still a busy one."•

They descended to the lower floor again. Mrs. Myner conducted her guest to a hack room, divided from the receptitin room by an unusually thick partition. __This back room was tile scliool room. It was long, and was provided with desks and benches. At one end of the room, upon a raised plea:urn, was the desk of the head teacher. And here with a single tallow candle burning dimly at his elbow, sat M. Myner, engaged in writing copies. He arose at the entrance of his wife with her guest. He was tall, of spare ggure, round shouldered and stooping, with a careworn visage. He wore spec- tacles, and his aspect was scholarly. He walked with a es ham bl in g gait, and his garments were almost threadbare. The world had evidently frowned upon the Rev. Thomas Myner, but there was no shadow of repining upon his rugged features: Like his wife, he was patient and hopeful under all dis- couragements. "Thomas," said Mrs. Myner, "this is the little pupil of whom I have spoken to you so often—the little girl heft to marry y0u.7.-Miss.Gwen Winter."

" I -am glad to see you, Miss Winter," said Mr. Myner, cordially shaking hands with her. "Yo' are welcome here. I have heard so much of you that I feel already acquainted with you." He placed chairs for her and his wife, and then, running his fingers through his sparse locks, he resumed his own seat. -

"Miss Gwen is to be a daughter to us, Thomas," said Mrs. Myner. "She puts herself in our hands. I hope that we may be able to keep her With us. She is too young to go out into the world." "Strange," said the curate, regarding Gwen re- flectively. "Miss Winter reminds me strongly of some one I have seen or known. I could almost be- lieve that I have seen you before, Miss Winter. The likeness of yours to another face that I have seen grows upon me. Yet I cannot remember." He passed his hand over his forehead wearily. "I never saw a face like Gwen's," said Mrs. Myner, eagerly. "Oh, I wish you could remem- ber, Thomas."

wish I could ; but I can't," said Mr. Myner. "I fancy that if I could remember we might have some clue to all this mystery you've been telling me about, my dear. It may come to me—the name I mean. But it eludes me now. Let us talk of Miss Gwen and her future. What are we to with her ?"

THE MURDER OF SIR JOHN BOTELER,

AT BEWSEY HALL.

Writing in the "Newcastle Weekly Chronicle,”Mr. W. L. Fleetwood says :—During the 15th and part of the 16th century, a powerful Lancashire family was tat of Boteler, or Botcher, also sometimes spelt Butteler or Butler. A distinguished family, the De Botelers, seems to have been once settled at or near Upton-on-Severn, in Worcestershire. In the old Parish Church of Upton-on-Severn, before its so- called restoration sometime in the last century, was the tomb of a knightly Be Boteller, belonging to this once potent family. In days ion.. gone by, the Botelers were summoned to Parliament as Barons of Warrington. One of them, Sir John Boteler, married a daughter of Sir John Savile, whose name was Anna or Anne. This lady appears not to have been at all of a humble spirit, but to have had a high idea of her own and her husband's dignity. The King being about to pay Lord Derby a visit at Lathom House, Lord Derby sent a message round to all his friends and dependents, desiring them to appear in his livery at Lathorn to do honour to his Royal guest. It was not in those days considered a degradation fsr gentlemen of importance even to wear the livery of some great nobleman. At the period of which lam writing, the livery more nearly resembled what we should now call a uniform ; for instance, long after this, at the com- mencement of the war between Charles I. and Parlia- ment, the regiments on both sides frequently wore the livery of the noblemen and gentlemen who raised them. The" Green Coats "of Hampden were celebrated on the Parliamentary side, and the" White Coats" of New- castle on that of the King. It was Cromwell who, by clothing his musketeers in a sort of brick-red colour, first introduced, as it were, a general unifotm. Lady Boteler, however, was highly enraged when she received Lord Derby's message at Bewsey Hall, and at once returned a haughty answer, to the effect." That she con- sidered her husband, Sir John Boteler, as suitable a person to entertain the King as any earl." This answer greatly incensed Lord Derby, who appears from this time farward to have annoyed the Botelers by every means in his power ; while they, on their part, were not slow to return the compliment whenever they get the chance.

The ferry across the Mersey at Warrington belonged to Sir John Beteler, and one day when the Ea' of Derby was going to London, and wished to cross by this ferry, Sir John refused to allow him to do so ; this enraged Lord Derby very much, as he was thus compelled to go round by Manchester. To prevent the like occurring again, and also to punish Sir John by depriving him of his tolls, the Earl built a bridge across the Mersey, and declared it free to everybody. But Sir John had no in- tention of being, as he considered, " done " in that manner ; he declared that he had a legal right to tolls on all passengers crossing the river, and that he was determined, whether they crossed by the ferry or crossed by the biidge, they should pay his toll just the same. Then Lord Derby appealed to the Sing, who decided that the bridge should be free. If this decision was pleasing to the Stanleys, it was in an equal degree displeasing to Sir John and his high-spirited wife, the Lady Anna ; so that the system of each family giving the other as much annoyance as possible probably went on much the same as before.

Perhaps Lord Derby on some occasion, or occasions, may have got the worst of it ; at all events, he appears to have resolved to end matters by securing the death of his adversary. He selected for his agent Sir Piers Legh—an ancestor of the Leghs, of Lyme Hall (from which that beautiful breed of dogs, the " Lyme-nsas- tiffs," take their name), near Stockport—and William Savage, both gentlemen of good Cheshire families. Legh and Savage bribed Sir John Boteler's porter and one of his other servants—they appear to have done this with- out much difficulty. These two traitors placed a light in one of the windows of Bewsey Hall as a signal to inform Sir Piers Legh and his partner in guilt when the murder might be most easily accomplished. Then when they saw the signal-taper, Legh and Savage crossed over the moat that surrounded the hall in a coracle (a hot' made of wicker covered with skins), and were, on land- ing, conducted by the two treacherous servants to their master's apartment. Here a fierce struggle took place before they could accomplish their fell purpose, for a faithful chamberlain, who slept in the ante-room to Sir John's bedroom, made a desperate resistance. However Le and Sir John were both murdered. The assassins would, probably, have killed the infant heir, too, but a faithful Negress carried the child away hidden in her apron. Legh and Savage now decamped, taking with them one of the faithless servants, whom they hanged on a tree to prevent his ming able to turn informer against them. History does not record what became of his fellow traitor, the other treacherous servant.

Sir Piers Legh and Savage never appear to have received in this life the due punishment of their guilt, for, though Lads' Boteler prosecuted them, her second husband Lord Grey, disallowed her suit ; at which she was so highly indignant that she left him and returned to Lancashire. saying, •• If my new husband, Lord Grey will not let me have my will of my dead lord's enemies, yet shall my body be buried by his kSir John Boteler's. The Legh family are still patrons of Disley Church, near Stockport. Legend accounts for their being patrons of this church because, Sir Piers Legh, being in holy orders, it was not easy for a secular court to punish him as he deserved ,or his share in the murder of Sir John Botaler ;so, according to the legend, he was condemned to build Risley Church as a penance. The murder, I believe, took place in the reign of Henry VII. ; But Sir Piers Legh did not complete the building of Disley Church till 1527, which would be in the reign of Henry

In Warrington Church, in the Bewsey chapel, is a splendid tomb to one of the Boteler family ; there is no inscription on it, but it is believed to be the tomb of Sir Thomas Boteler—Sir John's heir that was saved by the faithful black woman. On it are two reclining effi.ies, supposed to be those of Sir Thomas and his wife,holding each other's hands ; he in armour, she wearing a curious mitre-shaped cap. Noar it, under an arch in the wall, used to be a statue of the faithful Negress who ran away with Sir Thomas in her apron, and so saved his life. Bevrsey Hall, where the murder took place, is, I believe, still in existence, about a mile out of Warrington, so that their old house has survived the now extinct Bote- ler family. The family name of the Dukes of Ormond used to be Butler ; but whether that great Irish house was in any way connected with the Lancashire or Worcestershire Botelers, Botellers, Buttellers, or Butlers, I cannot say.

LANCASAIRE FARMERS AND "COW BEEF." FOR PAUPERS.

A meeting of the Federation of Lancashire Farmers' Associations was held at the Blackburn Technical School on Saturday afternoon, at which the president (Mr. Jas. 'ay, of Blackburn) proposed the following resolu- tion That, as the guardians of the Preston Union have agreed to accept tenders for good cow beef in lieu of heifer beef, thereby saving the union something like £5OO a year, this federation respectfully tenders its acknowledgement to them for such a sensible arrange- ment, and trusts every union in the country will follow.l its example." H. observed that, although he sympa- thised with the poor, he thought that goad cow beef was good enough for the persons who went into the worktfouse. People who stayed outside were glad to get it. It would also be a great saving to the union, as cow beef was 2d. cheaper than heifer beef.—Mr. Slater (Blackburn) seconded the motion, and said that many farmers were glad to eat cow beef, and he did not see why they should supply heifer beef to paupers.—Mr. Whitworth (Bury) thought it was quite unreasonable to provide heifer beef for paupers.—The motion was car- ried.

TOWN HALL, PARK, AND BATHS COMMITTEE.

Alderman CIIANDLEY, in moving the adoption of this Committee's minutes %%MON were published in the " Examiner" last week, said that there had been a trial of the water supply for the new baths. They had had a large bath—not the largest one—filled. They had the choice of Appleton or Winwick water, and had chosen the Appleton supply.. He was sure the water was free from all impunity, although in consequence of the shadows and the coloured roof it had a peculiar yellow- ish-green hue. He wished to point out to bathers that this had nothing whatever to do with the nature of the water, which when put into small vessels was of a bright clear character. In the bath which he saw filled they tried the clearness of the water by placing a penny in the six feet end, and it was possible to decipher the head and tail of the coin which was a satisfactory proof of the clearness of the water. (Hear, hear.) They would ob- tain the water from Appleton at a much cheaper rate, and they would, therefore, be able to renew the supply in the baths much oftener than formerly—(hear, hear)—than they could with the Winwick water. He then read the analysis of the water which had been recently made and which snowed that the Appleton water is slightly peaty and contains vegetable matter; it contains a considerable quantity of organic matter of vegetable origin, but it contained nothing of the nature of sewage and is suitable for domestic use. The fact of it having been pronounced fit for domestic use should be a guarantee of its purity. (Hear, hear.) In addition to this the water was of a much warmer temperature than the Winwick water, which was obtained from a much lower strata. Councillor FLoon said he had been informed that the Committee were about to discharge the present manager of the baths. When they came to consider that Brad- shaw had been with them for 16 years, he thought they would be justified in asking the Committee for some specific reason for their action. He did not object to the Committee's right to make a change but he asked them to consider the manager's past services. Alderman CHANDLEY : I know nothing whatever of the matter, as the Chairman of the Committee. I think it was rather discourtious on the port of the Committee to take such an action without acquainting their Chair- man with it. (Hear, hear.) Councillor ROEKRTS also asked for information, and Councillor BOLTON said that if the Chairman had taken the touble to have attended the meeting he would be aware of the reasons. The Committee considered that it required a man and his wife and a boy to look after the baths. At present they had only a.rnan. Councillor ROBERTS said that, while giving the Cam- mittee credit for the reasons that had actuated them, it seemed hard that a man who had been 16 years in the Corporation should be so summarily dismissed. (Hear. hear.) Bradshaw was now at a time of life when he was beyond taking up active work, and the consequence was if he had no means he would have to come upon the rates of the town for support. He only mentioned this because he believed that it was generally the cus- tom of the Corporation to pension its old servants. They were able to keep a consulting ex-gas manager at a nominal figue, and also a consulting head gardener at a nominal figure, and why should they not go in for a consulting bath's manager. (Laughter and hear, hear.) He merely mentioned this as one way out of the difficulty. (Hear, hear, and laughter.) Councillor R. FlEaTon said that Bradshaw had ceased years ago to be what the Corporation stipulated for when they engaged him. The agreement was that the manager should have the assistance of a wife; their manager had been a widower for some years. Councillor SMETHURST : Can the Committee give us any idea when the new baths wiil be opened. Alderman CHANDLEY : I think it will be five or six months. (Several voices "You mean weeks.") No I mean months. Alderman Surron : It will be opened in winter then. (Laughter.) The Town CLERK (to Alderman Chandley) : I think it will be about six weeks. Alderman CHANDLEY : I hope so ; but I am inclined to doubt it. With regard to the dismissal of the baths' manager I think something ought to be done in the way of providing him furtheremployment under the Corpora- tion. (Hear, hear.) The minutes were then put and carried. MARKETS COMMITTEE. The minutes of this Committee stated that the total income from all sources for the past four weeks was £283 18s. 3d. ; corresponding period of last year, £274 lie. 9d. ; increase £9 6.. 6d. It was also reported that Mr. Crabtree, the assistant inspector of weights and measures, had successfully passed the examination of the Board of Trade. The MAYOR proposed, Alderman RLACKHURST seconded the adoption of the minutes, which were accepted. OAS COMMITTEE.—" ANOTHER SCENE."

. - Councillor R.T.FaincLonon in submitting the minutes of this Committee for adoption pointed out that the ten- der for 1,000 tons of cannel was solicited, so as to get in a supply which would remove the possible danger of the town being left in darkness some night. Councillor Bourori wanted to know whether some- thing could not be done to prevent the awful stench which emanated from the gas works in Mersey-st., and permeated the neighbourhood. Everyone in the neigh- bourhood had been suffering from head ache for weeks through it, and it appeared to him to be nothing but the result of bad management. (Hear, hear.) Councillor DixoN disagreed with the expenditure of £1,048 for a new gas hobier, and wanted to know why the prices were not given in the tender which it was pro- posed to accept from the Stanton Iron Wcrks Company for the supply of cast iron gas pipes and castings. Councillor &raw thought the Chairman of the mittee was not doing his duty in omitting to give parti- culars of prices and quantities of the tender for pipes and also the price of the tender for cannel. Councillor BROWNE agreed with Councillor Shaw and he considered they were entirely left in the dark on the subject. They did not know what they were voting for. He also wished to ask whether the tenders had been advertised for, in accordance with the standing resslu- tion of the Council, which made it imperative on every committee to advertise for tenders over £2O. He also agreed with Councillor Bolton's complaint respecting the stench in Mersey-st. Councillor ROBERTS moved as an amendment that the minutes with the exception of those relating to the tenders tor the coal and the cast iron pipes be con- firmed. He took that course in order to ascertain whether tenders had been solicited for the cannel and cast iron pipes, and if so he would know what quantitv of coal and how many castings had been purchased, and at what price. With regard to the gasholder, he expressed his surprise that the committee had decided to purchase a new holder, for it was generally understood that with the centralization of the manufacturing plant at Longford all the works at Mersey-st. would be aban- doned. Councillor R. T. FAIRCOUGH informed Councillor Roberts that it was intended to manufacture all the gas at Lpngford, but it was thought desirable to keep on the storage reservoirs at Mersey-st. Councillor DIXON seconded the amendment.

Councillor ROBERTS said that Councillor Fairclough's explanation with regard to the new holder was satisfac- tory enough, that was if thecommittee had only the ob- jects he had stated in view. Alderman SUTTON said that it was an utter impossibi- lity for every committee to give details of everything that came before it. Every member of the Council had an opportunity of obtaining information he required by re- ferring to the minute books, and asking for particulars before the Council ; consequently he did not see any harm in suppressing these particulars from the agenda paper. 'Alderman CHANDLEY said he would like to take advan- tage of the opportunity for saying how inconvenient it was to publish details of contracts in the notices issued to the members of the Corporation. There was no , reason why, as Alderman Sutton had observed, if any member of the Council desired to ascertain the condi- tions under which a contract had been let, and the nature and prices of it, he should not apply at the Gas Works. There, every information would be given to him. It was very objectionable indeed to have all the details of contracts and prices published in the agendas, which came before the notice of the general public, and conse- quently did them (the Gas Committee) much harm in one sense in that direction. Alderman HOLMES concurred with the remarks of Aid. Chandley. Councillor SHAW pointed out that the prices of goods tendered by several local traders were appended on the agenda paper. Why any difference should be made be- tween large and small manufacturers he was utterly at a loss to 'CROW. Councillor R. T. FAIRCLOUGH said that tenders had been solicited for the castings, but he could not tell him what the prices were. A tender was also solicited for the cannel. In reply to Councillor Shaw, he could only say that were the Council to publish the amounts of the tenders of large firms, they would in the future refuse to submit tenders. Councillor ROBERTS wanted to be informed explicitly whether the tenders had been advertised in the manner prescribed by resolution of the Council. If they had been, he did not intend to press the amendment. He only wanted a plain answer. If the tenders had been asked for in the proper way. Councillor R. T. FaincLouon replied that the tenders for the gas pines were advertised for, but with respect to the cannel that was not the case. A special kind of cannel was required and it was decided to ask for special prices. A special quotation was given, and so much re- duction was obtained. Councillor ROBERTS : It is a most unbusinesslike pro- ceeding. Councillor JAMES FAIRCLOUGH : You do it your- self. Councillor ROBERTS : It is a different thing when you are dealing with the public money. Councillor JAMES FAIRCLOUGH : You had better take my place then. Councillor ROBERTS : I can't do that. Councillor Roberts then proceeded to again refer to the manner in which the contracts were given, when Councillor JAMES FAIRCLOUGH said that it was all " rubbish !"

Councillor ROBERTS : If you want to argue the point out, get up in your place and do it. The MAYOR : Do you persist in pressing your amend- ment? Councillor ROBERTS said that he would do so. The voting was then taken, and for the amendment 14 voted and 10 against it. The amendment was declared carried, and the minutes in the amended form were then put and carried. Councillor R. T. THE ADVERTISING OF TENDERS.-A MOTION OF RESCINDMENT. •

Councillor R. T. FAIRCLOTIGIi moved that the following resolution, passed by the Council at a meeting held on the sth April, 1887, be rescinded, viz. :—" That all committees of this Council shall be required for the , future to produce the whole of their requirements, whether for materials to be supplied or labour to be per- fortned, or both, by tender, and that whenever the genie' shall involve an expenditure of £2O and upwards. teni- ders be publicly advertised for, and that every accepted ' tender shall be published with the minutes of the com- mittee connected therewith, together with the price and the name of the successful firm." He wished to show the Council that the Gas Committee of the town were large buyers, manufacturers, and sellers, just the same as were owners of other large works, and he did not perceive that any one of these works found it to be to their interests to be bound to advertise tor anything they required of the value of £2O. Gas works are continuous or automatic, and gas must be provided at all costs. Thei committee, therefore, submitted that if any disarrange- ment occurred in the works and the resolution was not rescinded or altered, they might find themselves in dark- ness when they least expected it. He considered the Gas Committee should hold positions similar to directors in public companies only without pay, and 0 show them the absurdity of this resolution he would give them an illustration. Could any member realize the position of Councillor Roberts if the shareholders of the Warring- ton Slate Co. passed a similar resolution, that he must not buy anything of the value of £2O without first advertising far tenders? Would not the profits be infinitesimal ? _ _

Councillor ROBERTS rose to a point of order. This was not a personal matter, and he did not see the comparison between "the shareholders of the Warrington Slate Company " and the Corporation. Councillor R. T. FAIRCLOUGH : Very well then, we will say Messrs. Rylands or Messrs. Crosfield ; if they advertised for tenders for £2O where would their chance of profit be ? It would be infinitesimal. I cannot see the wisdom of keeping a resolution of this character upon the Standing Orders—a resolution which cannot be adhered to without a very serious loss to the town. I Councillor JAMES FAIRCLOUGH seconded the motion. Councillor ROBRRTS was sorry Councillor R. T. Fair- dough had not something better to say in support of his proposition. They had been told there was a possibility of the town being in darkness some day because of the operation of the resolution, but it had been in operation six years and they had never yet been in darkness. (A Voice : "Very nearly.") They had been in partial dark- ness, but not in consequence of the operation of the re- solution, and when their lights went out in consequence of the resolution it would be time for Councillor Fair- dough to speak out. No one would refuse to support the action of the Gas Committee with regard to the supply of material in caso of an unexpected difficulty. (Hear, hear.) The comparison which Councillor Fair- dough had drawn between Corporations and private firms was palpable on the face of it. Private firms were only responsible to the shareholders, but Corporations were responsible to the whole of the ratepayers. No case had been made out why the resolution should be re- scinded. He alluded to the Salford gas scandals as an instance of the result of allowing public business to be done secretly. (Hear, hear.) Councillor Roberts, in conclusion, contended that every ratepayer in Warrington had a perfect right to know how tne money he subscribed for was expended. (Hear, ' hear.) Councillor BOLTON had never seen any reason why the resolution should be rescinded. Alderman Sarrox pointed out that there were always cases of emergency arising when committees should have full powers entrusted to them with regard to tenders. He did not think the motion of Councillor Fairclough went far enough. He suggested that it should be with- drawn, and some alternative scheme introduced,and the Council might consider the two together. Councillor BROWSE opposed the motion ; and said he agreed with Councillor Roberts that Councillor Fair. dough had shown nothing whatever on which to found Iris motion. Councillor FLOOD contended that some protection should be afforded to the ratepayers, who had all the money to find. Councillor DIXON was puzzled to know why there was always so much secrecy connected with the proceedings of th\e Warrington Gas Committee. Contracts were advertised by other Committees. Alderman WRIGHT said it had been found that by " striking while the iron was hot" they could buy a cargo of setts at a very satisfactory price, and thus save the ratepayers and property owners a considerable per- centage. Let Councillor Fairclough withdraw his motion and submit a modified one. Councillors HEATON and BEAstAx spoke on the motion and

Alderman RLACKEIIIRST said he would make the reso- lution more stringent, and apply it to every committee of the Council for every article above a certain amount. Councillor Snew said that in doing work for the Corporation he had been asked for tenders for contracts I very much below £2O. Councillor B. T. Fartict.ountr, in reply, said that he would just like the Council to thoroughly understand that the Gas Committee always asked for ten- ders when they possibly could. He thought that the chairman and committee should be relieved from the responsibility of a resolution of that character. Mr. Browne had talked about sensible persons, but here there was a sensible proposition on the agenda arranging for a purchase of cannel, but the same sensible people had refused to pass it. They would rather do without gas. Could they do without it ? It was like being between "the devil and the deep blue sea," to be between the Council and the Chairman that night. (Loud laughter.) The resolution as it at present stood on the books of the Corporation was an unworkable one, and they could not carry on the work with a resolution of its description. He was willing to have the resolution he proposed amended if it did not altogether coincide with the views of the Council. Coun- cillor Fairclough, however, finally withdrew the resolu- tion, promising to bring forward the question at another meeting. Councillor ROBERTS lam sorry that the motion was withdrawn. I would rather have had the matter decided to-night. The Council then rose.

FINANCE COMMITTEE.-THE RATES.

The minutes of this committee referred to the levying of an improvement rate of 4s. in the pound, and a sewer rate of 2d. in the pound. The summary of estimates is as follows :—Borough rate, £9,534 85. 4J.; improvement rate, £27,063 155. 6d.; general sewers rate, £1,511 15s. ld. ; or a total of £38,019 7s. 11d. Alderman HOLMES moved the confirmation of the minutes of the committee. Councillor SHAW G tams' seconded the motion. THII COST OF THE SMALLPDX EPIDEMIC.

---- -- Councillor ROBERTS said that for the purpose of ob- taining the opinion of the Council on the question of the rates, he would move an amendment to the effect that the minutes of the committee be anted to with the ex- ception of the resolutions on pages 2'l and 22, one of the items including a sum of £7,550 es. 11d, on account of the smallpox epidemic. Was it not possible and legal, and even wise, to spread the cost over two years instead of over one? ("Hear, hear "and "No, no.") Ordinarily speaking he believed they should pay as they went on; but, considering such an extraordinary expense, the terrible results of the epidemic, and the terrible loss to trade, it would, he contended, be judieious to spread the repayment of the cost over two years. (Hear, hear.) t Councillor SnAvr seconded the amendment. Councillor SLIAW-GRERN said a generation had elapsed since they had passed through such an epidemic in War- rington, and the sum was a fair one to distribute over two years. The epidemic had come upon them as a visitation from the Almighty, and he believed they would Only be acting fairly to the ratepayers, who had been heavily taxed, if they agreed with the suggestion which had been made by Councillor Roberts. Councillor DIXON and Councillor BOLTON supported the amendment. Alderman Flormcs said the Committee were almost unanimous in recommending the payment in one year. There were large expenses looming in the distance. They had recently spent £25,000 ia the purchase of property ; the interest would have to be paid as well as the sinking fund, and they would also have to go in for the widen- ing of Bridge-st., a matter which could not be avoided. He had always found that if they avoided expense one year other expenses cropped up which were not ex- pected. It was, he believed, prudent to pay the expenses of the current year. Alderman CBANDLEY : The debt is not completed yet. The amendment was carried by 14 votes to 11. The other minutes of the Committee were then passed. Councillor Saaw GREEN asked if they would be com- pelled to postpone for a whole month the making and levying of any rate? Could they not arrange the amount now ?

PA. LUSH -AND DISTRICT COUNCILS.

A PAPER BY THE CLERK TO TIIE WARRING- TON UNION.

At the monthly meeting of the Warrington Board , of Guardians held on Wednesday, in the Town Hall Mr. Sutton, Clerk to the Board, read a paper on Parish and District Councils. He said he had been requested to explain to the Board the changes from the present system of Local Government that will follow if the main principles of the Parish and District Councils Bill now before Parliament, as they will be applicable to the Union. A copy of the Bill only came to him that week,hence ia conveyina to the Board his first impressions of a first reading, it should be un- derstood that he might in some respects have failed to grasp, or misinterpreted the full intention of the Govern- ment. .Upon similar changes being attempted and achieved on past occasions,he had remarked how pecu- liarly, yet apparently without purpose or intentioa, the new thing had corresponded to the very old. Comparing the provisions of the present Bill with the history of early life in English villages it would evidence in many ways a revival thereof. The Bill was not alt-igether of the kind they had expected. They had assumed that, taking the Parish Council as the unit, the District Coun- cil as the superior authority and the County Council as the still higher power, there would, be absorbed, in the three the whole system of Parish, Union, and County Government. It was not quite so, however, except that the duties of Highway Boards were to be given to the District Council and that a number of things now done by county magistrates were hereafter to form part of the work of the District Council, no material departure from prevailing proce- dure was contemplated. If they assumed the Bill to have passed, the position would be this :—Each parish would be a civil and not an ecclesiastical parish, and would consist of at least 800 inhabitants, or it would be grouped with the next parish. They had two such parishes in that union, viz : Cuerdley and Houghton. It would also mean a parish separated from an Urban dis- trict and, thus, the two extra municipal parts of War- rington and Latchford would each be a separate parish. These parishes would meet annually. That was to say men and women, whose names were on .the parliament- ary and County Council lists would meet at a time to be fixed from six to eight p.m., and they would appoint a chairman who would be chairman for the year. Far such meetings at reasonable periods they would have the free use of any public or elementary school. The parish expenses would be charged upon the poor-rate. They would elect parish councillors to a number vary- ing from five to 15, according as might be fixed by the County Council. The Parish Council would appoint overseers and assistant overseers. The Clerk might he a member of the Council without remuneration, or the assistant overseer or other fit person with a salary to be approved by the District Council. All property at pre- sent vested in overseers or churchwardens would be vested in the Parish Council and also the powers and duties of vestry churchwardens and overseers. It ought however, to be remembered that the transfer did not in- clude purely church property or church business. The church would be left to deal with its own property and affairs as hitherto. The parish meeting might adopt such Acts as the lighting and watching, the baths and wash-houses the burial, and public libraries, &c., and when adopted the Parish Council would deal with them. The Parish Council might acquire land and buildings for public purposes, and recreation grounds, and for public walks. They might deal with ponds and pools, and might acquire rights of way, easements, or °the" rights. They will held gifts of property and effect parish im- provements. It is an important, and may prove a bene- ficial power to the Parish Council to utilise any water supply therein for the use of the inhabitants. Their opening powers are, however, limited. The parish meeting must consent to any expense that will involve a loan, or that will involve in any one year a rate that will exceed one penny in the £. The District Council must, moreover, consent to any liability or expense in- volving that penny rate. If such consent be ref used,the County Council may be appealed to, and their decision will be final. All the expenses of a parish Council will be defrayed out of the poor rate. He would now proceed to the District Council and to the Board of Guardians. By almost universal consent, the administration of the poor law will remain solely with the Board of Guardians. The Guardians will cease to appoint the Rural Sanitary Authority. Each Urban Authority will become an Urban District Council. Each Rural Sanitary Authority will become a Rural District Council with urban and highway powers added to their present labours. The county magistrates will no longer be ex-officio Guardians. Guardians are to be elected for three years subject to a third of the Board retiring an- nually. They will be elected by ballot on the one man one vote (one woman one vote) principle. No qualifi- cation of any kind will be required. Men or women may be so elected. The rural District Councillors will number equally with guaidians for the Rural District, and each councillor elected to that office, will be the I guardian also for the parish. The magistrate will cease to grant certain kinds of licenses, and to exercise certain other duties, and the administration thereof, will de- volve upon the District Council. The chairman of each District Council may, during the period of his office act as a county magistrate. It is unnecessary to dwell further upon the District Council and the Board of Guardians for, save as to enlarged powers, the new order of things may be viewed in the main to correspond closely with the old. The County Councils are in several important ways to be the superior Authority. Included with their powers will be the alteration of areas and boundaries. He had to express, if he rightly read the provisions of the bill as to areas and boundaries, much personal satisfaction with the departure now to be made from the purposes of the inquiries as to county and union boundaries, instituted prior to the passing of the County Government Act. Many on that Board would remember that it was then contemplated that the whole of a poor law union, must be brought within one county. Hence they strove very hard at each and every point to bring their Che- shire parishes into the county of Lancashire as in very olden times they had been. Without entering into the many reasons for offering strenuous opposition to any curtailment or the area of their union, he would remind them their contention was that to take from the union the Cheshire portions, would be, for financial reasons and for causes affecting the best interests of the poor, a great wrong. Contrary to reasonable expectation, and to the judgment of the able assistant commissioner who held the local inquiry, the decision of the commissioners was, save as to the urban or municipal part of Latchford against them. Thereupon in every available form and way and in very strong terms, the Board protested against that decision and raised the further objection that no real necessity existed for so altering the bounda- ries of unions, and that they had net the least objection to offer any alteration of the boundary of the Rural Sanitary District so as to bring the areas thereof within their several counties. If he correctly understood the bill the unions need not be narrowed so as to be in one county. Yet the District Council must be so. The difficulty thereon suggesting itselt to his mind related to the expediency of constituting so small an area as the Parish of Grappenhall, Thelwall, and the extra munici- pal parts of Latchford into that of a Rural District Council. It might become a question for their serious considera- tion, and for the authority of the two County Councils

to be exercised upon, whether as was contemplated when the other Cheshire parishes were first annexed to to that Union the township of Appleton might not for poor-law purposes be joined to the Warrington Union, thus enabling a convenient rural district of reasons ble area to be formed of the Cheshire portion and leaving the county boundaries untouched. He would also urge upon the board and particularly upon the Guardians for the rural parishes the desirability of giving close atten- tion to the Parliamentary procedure in relation to the

THE EDUCATION CRISIS AT ST HELENS.

VOLUNTARY VERSUS BOARD SCHOOLS. --- IMMEDIATE AND DECISIVE ACTION NECESSARY. The ratepayers of St. Helens are face to face with the question of either at once raising £7,000, and continuing the voluntary system of elementary education, or establishing a school board in that borough. The vicar of St. Helens (the Rev. J. W. Willink, MA.) has just issued a circular convening a conference to be held at the Town in which he says :—" In view of the grave crisis which has arisen in the town in connection with elementary education, I have been requested by Colonel Gamble, Mr. Sinclair, Colonel Richard Pilkingten, the directors of Messrs. R: Evans and Co., and others, who are keenly interested in the matter, and all agreed as to the supreme importance, of taking such immediate steps as shall render the formation of a school board for St. Helens an impossibility, to invite your attendance, the object being to take the subject of local voluntary education into consideration, and, if possible, to agree upon some course of action by which the necessary money may be raised, and the whole question placed upon a sure foundation for the future." A PUNGENT CRITICISM.

The following letter has appeared in -the Liverpool " Daily Post "dealing with the " crisis" :—Sir —You published yesterday the fact that the Vicar of Sc. 'Helens is appealing to the public for £7,000 to supply deficien- cies in the means for public elementaery education, with the object of staving off a school board for the town, and keeping in the hands of a self-elected few the con- ' trol of great sums of public money. Your brilliant article of yesterday on the subject of public education, strongly condemnatory as it was of the policy of tha vicar of St. Helens :and his supporters, did very natur- ally not touch upon this specific case, and therefore, I propose, with your permission, to address a few words Upon the subect to the the people of St. Helens and to the vast circle of your readers generally. The latest Parliamentary return I have by me at the moment upon the matter in hand is tor the year ending August 31, 1889. In this return it appears that there were in St. Helens and Parr fifteen public elementary schools, two British, two Wesleyans, bur Church of England, and six Roman Catholic, besides ene Small unclosed school with an average attendance of eighty scholars at the Crown Glass Works. The manarrs of these fifteen schools dispensed in that year a total income of £10,856, out of which it is stated that £1,676 only came from subscrip- tions. Though this is an unusually large proportion, and showing the generosity of the subscribers in a very favourable light, it cannot be said to be so large as to entitle them to the sole control of the schools. An in- teresting fact as to these subscriptions is that only £152 9s. is set down as contributed to the Church of England schools, less than one-tenth of the total, though they contained just one-fourth of the scholars in average attendance. This fact, showing that he has almost a virgin field to work upon may give encouragement to the Vicar in his efforts to raise the thousands he wants. Sik:of the fifteen schools are, as I have said, Catholic echo& St. Helens voted at the last general election,by a small majority,and as I think mistakenly,arinst what is called the domination of the priests in Ireland. The Vicar and his supporters are,however,all for the domination of the priests in St. Helens, We Liberals, sir, favour con- trol by the people who pay, and we would welcome priest and parson alike to our schools, each to teach his own, provided he came not to command, but as one desiring to serve, and as such deserving of honour.— Yours, dm., JOHN T. BRUNNER. Druids' Cross, Wavertree, April 4, 1893.

THE HOME RULE BILL IN PARLIAMENT.

The second reading of the Home Rule Bill was moved in Parliament on Thursday by Mr. Gladstone, who after an appeal to the results of autonomy in foreign coun- tries and a reference to the dissaffection of our colonies when they were governed from Downing-street as Ireland is governed from Dublin Castle. referred to the tears which had been expressed as to the Irish members at Westminister taking part in votes of confidence upon questions substantially English or British, and said that of all the twelve vetes of confidence in the last sixty years there had not been one upon a British question—eight of them were upon Imperial, and four upon Irish questions. He thought these eighty members would be found at Westminster upon proper occasions, and that those occasions would be somewhat rare. There had, he held, been nothing like habitual inconvenience from the interference of Irish members in British questions. As to the financiatmatter, he did not believe that a financial adjustment between the two countries could be made in any manner which would be entirely free from incoavenience, but all that inconvenience was mere dust in the balance when compared with the importance of the great purpose they had in view—the real union of the countries and the consolidation of the Empire. Sir Michael Hicks-Beach moved the rejection of the bill. He contended that it neither gave real autonomy to Ireland in purely Irish affairs nor secured the supremacy of the Imperial Parliament. It left the landlords without protection, and its safeguards for the minority were absolutely unreal, and its securities to the British taxpayer a sham. The debate was adjourned at midnight.

On Monday a serious accident occurred on the Hay. dock section of the Liverpool, St. Helens, and South Lancashire Railway. An engine was taking a train of empty wagons along the line to the steam navvy, when Thomas Wilson, twenty-two, a labourer of Golborne, who was travelling in the same direction, attempted to get on the engine. He missed the step and both his feet were caught by the connecting rod and severely crushed. The engine was immediately stopped, and Wilson ex- tracted. He was taken to the Haydock Cottage Hospi- tal about 100 yards away,and attended by Dr. Hayward. Both feet were very badly crushed.

1211 E SHOCKING DIsCOVER.Y IN A.

WIDNES HOTEL.

Mr. S. Brighouse, the county coroner, on Wednes- day held an inquest at the Widnes Police Court, on the body of a newly-born female child, which was found concealed in the lumber room of the Com- mercial Hotel, Appleton, on the 2.3 rd ult. Mary Sweeney, a native of Tralee, County Kerry, the mother of the infant, was present in custody, and she was represented by kir.J.W, Knowles. Inspector Pattinnon conducted the proceedings on behalf of the police.—Dr.J.lkl. MacLennan deposed that he had made a post-mortem examination, and be described the body as well nourished and fully-developed. He was of opinion that the child had had a separate existence from the mother,and that death was due to asphyxia,caused by forcible obstruction of the breath- ing. In reply to the Coroner, witness said he put aside any accidental suffocation during birth, when he said violence had been used. Considerable vio- lence must have been used to have caused the clots on the brain. —Samuel Hurst, licensed victualler, Commercial Hotel, said the prisoner came to his house on the 20th ult., she was engaged as domestic servant. On the 23rd his suspicions were aroused, and he went to the prisoner's bedside, and she admitted that she had given birth to a child. She told him where she had put it, and the police found the body at the place indicated. The child was wrapped in a cloth, and outside this there was a string round-the , child's neck. The string was not tied.—Beatrice Hurst, • daughter of last witness, said she awoke at six o'clock on Wednesday morning, and she saw Mary Sweeney dressed and kneeling on the floor. Witness thought she heard a child cry. Mary Sweeney asked her to get up and light the fire as she did not feel well.—The Coroner, after consulting his hooks, directed the jury that the child had had a separate existence.—P.S. McGregor deposed to finding the body.—The prisoner volunteered to give evidence on oath, and after being cau- tioned, she stated that she was a married woman, the wife of Patrick Sweeney. Her husband was the father of the child. The child was bprn on the 22nd, and when she first saw it, it was dead. She did not hear it cry. She wrapped the body up and put it in the lumber room intending to have it buried. She did not know what she was doing. Her husband left her on the 22n1 August last to go to America, and she supposed he was there plow. It was not true she had been living with a men namPd Peter Murray. She had been married seven years. The Coroner, in summing up, said the verdict must be one of either wilful murder or an open verdict. After a quarter of an hour's considera- tion in private, the jury returned an open verdict.

BRASS BAND CONTEST AT ,RUNCORN.

A brass band contest, arranged for the benefit of the Runcorn Town and District Band, took place on the football field, Irwell-lane, on Saturday. Unfortunately the weather was wet but there was nevertheless a large number of spectator& Twenty six bands had entered the competition, and the whole of neva put in an appearance. It was after nine o'clock when the last band took the stand. The best piece was " Marina Faliero " (Wright and Round) and the jud:ze was Mr. A. D. Keate, of Manchester. his awards were as follows :-18c prize (.£l9 11k), Eccles Boro ; 2nd (Xl2 125.), Goodshaw ; 31,1 (0 944, Bradford (Manchester) ; 4th (.E6 6 Gossages' Widnes; sth (.£2), Verdin Adelaide, Nortlawich. The judge said they bad had a very good and close contest and several bands had given a really grand performance of the piece.
