TIIE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, APRIL 8, 1893.

===== 0002601_18930408_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
Demonstrations IT has been known for a long time in Belfast that there are a large number of . persons in the north of Ireland who are opposed to Home Rule. The demonstrations which have taken place this week in Belfast litive done little else than emphasize that fact. It is a question whether the result obtained is worth the expenditure that has been incurred. That money has been lavishly spent over the performance and that no element of success has been omitted which could be bought with hard cash, is evidenced by the fact that amongst the demonstrators, or at all events, arsongst the crowds which filled the streets of the

-rn

===== 0002601_18930408_art0092.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
.

===== 0002601_18930408_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATUEDAY, APRIL 8, 1893.

capital of Ulster, were a large number of " visi- tors" whose appearance on the scene was not, to say the least, entirely due to the promptings of a pure patriotism nor even to an unquenchable antipathy to Home Rule. These visitors were neither more nor less than a party of workingmen —carefully selected workingmen, we may be sure —variously numbered at from five to eight hundred I specially imported from the three northernmost English counties, Northumberland, Durham, and Cumberland. Their fares from and back to their native districts were paid, and their allowance, in addition, was fourteen shillings a day. We do not hear that any onerous obligations were imposed upon them in return for this liberal treatment, but no doubt they will be expected, out of pure gratitude if for no other reason, to enlarge upon the determination of what Lord RANDOLPH Cuußcium. calls the "stern and dour" folk of the North of Ireland not to submit to an Act of Parliament. There is in this country, however,a wholesome tendency to discount the value of bought testimony. Mr. BALFOUR who was, in the absence of his uncle the MARQUIS OF SALISBURYothO central figure of this Orange manifestation, con- demned the Government and their supporters for not recognizing facts. The fact which he wishes to have specially recognised is the hostility of a part of Ulster to the Home Rule scheme. There are however, other and more important facts which claim recognition, and it would be well for Mr. BALFOUR and all those who have been so loudly applauding his utterances to give attention to them. One of these facts is that a majority of at least two-thirds of the Irish people desire and demand Home Rule, and another fact is that, behind this two-thirds majority, there is a majority of the whole of the representatives of the United Kingdom who are convinced that in the interests alike of Ireland and the rest of the-king- dom, Home Rule should be conceded. The " facts " of the situation, then, which Mr. BALForra is so desirous to have recognised bring us face to face with the question whether the policy of the country is to be determined by a majority or by a minority. No one denies to the Ulster minority the right to demonstrate and to proclaim their desires or their grievances in the most forcible manner within the law. They have been invited time after time to state their objections to the Home Rule Bill of the Government, and to point out in what manner it might be altered to their advantage. The Government have displayed a disposition which has almost verged upon weak- ness to safeguard the interests of Irish minorities and propertied classes. -If further" safeguards " or modifications are required let them be formu- lated, and, judging from the past, we may be assured they will receive even more than an indul- gent consideration. What cannot be tolerated is that a minority should take up the unconstitu- tional position of declaring that because it objects to a certain measure therefore that measure shall not beconde law. The British constitution does not admit the right of a minority to absolutely veto legislation ; if it did so the era of pro- gress would be at an end, for there is no measure of reform which does not excite in some quarters a bitter and unrelenting opposition.

Teachers THE annual conference of the in National Union of Teachers has

Conference. -taken place this week at Liverpool, and much that was said and done at it is worth more than a passing notice from those interested in the educational affairs of the country. The address of the President, Mr. C. BOWDEN, of Gateshead, was replete with information and suggestion. A good deal of what he said was, of course, more of interest to teachers themselves than to outsiders, but, on the other hand, there was much that was interesting to the general public. It is, for ex- ample, pleasing tc be told by one so competent to express an opinion that there is less and less to be said each day, of the inferiority of English schools compared with those of the Continent, and that the latest of American writers confessed that r for thoroughness, accuracy, neatness and steady plodding work, English schools were better than those of America. We are told, further, that if a similar comparison were made with European schools the verdict would be the same. Yet, Eng- lish teachers and scholars have some special dia.. advantages to contend against, and one of these, according to Mr. BOWDEN, is the antiquated and complicated English system of weights and measures. The learning of this sys- tem, as compared with the simple decimal system, means "two years of useless drud- gery on the part of the teacher and of weariness and misery and often pain on the part of the scholar." We are told that if a change were made from the old to the new method of computation the arithmetic which now occupies three years in teaching could all ho done in one,at much less cost of time and trouble. With regard to the status and treatment of school teachers there is room for improvement. Some people are disposed to think that, next to the country parson, the village schoolmaster has attained to an ideal existence. But, according to the President of the National Union, this is a mistake—at all events in many cases. The village schoolmaster suffers badly from one-man management, the " one-man" generally being the other village notable—the parson. He is often required to do all kinds of extra work, mostly in connection with the church and choir, and this not as a favour but as a duty. This is not all. Some of the smaller School Boards are even worse offenders than the Rector or the Vicar. "Which of us," says Mr. BownEN, "would not prefer .to be dismissed from our positions as teachers because we would not play the organ in the church or attend the Sunday school, rather than because we refused to marry a petty School Board member's daughter, to trade or not to trade at a certain shop,or because we would not clean the schoolroom or whitewash the offices connected with it at the command of such a Board?" Everyone will agree that the school teacher should be protected alike from the imposi- tions of the clerical manager and the petty perse- cutions of ignorant and narrow-minded members of small School Boards.

It can hardly be said that Mr. BAL- " Stupid Majorities." FOUR was the right man in the right guage was npolta cbee ho bellicose s eenoughvisitt o Belfast.to s. t tHh ei s la veryn- pronounced taste of his Orange auditors, and consequently COLONEL SAUNDERSON came in for a large share of the most vociferous applause. Mr. BALFOUR has written philosophical books, and there is too much of the " pale cast of thought" about his utterances for men who like to talk about "lining the ditches." There is a fine swash- buckling ring about Lord RANDOLPH CHURCHILL'S phrase," Ulster will fight, and Ulster will be right," which Mr. BALFOUR cannot even attempt to emulate. The nearest he could approach to an incitement to rebellion, or rather to an attack upon the police, for that

is what the Ulster " revolt " will most probably culminate in, was what he termed an " abstract an,*l academic" proposition. He did not believe that the Ulster people ever would have to fight for their liberties, but he admitted " that the , tyranny of majorities might be as had as the tyranny of kings, and that the stupidity of majorities might be even greater than the stupid- ity of kings, and he would not say, and did not think any rational or sober man would say, that what was kistifiable against the tyrannical king might not under certain circumstances be justifi- able against & tyrannical majority." This somewhat halting and half-hearted justifica- tion of resistance to the rule of the majority under which we all live, was, we are told, received with" continued cheering and waving of hats." It was not followed,as it should have been,by another "abstract and academic proposition " defining which majorities are stupid and tyrannical—those majorities which seek to apply suitable remedies to palpable and intolerable social ailments,or those majorities which blindly and stubbornly resist all reform no matter how obvious is the need of change.

THEFT OF JEWELLERY AT RUNCORN. During Tuesday night the shop of Mr. Thomas Carr, watchmaker and jeweller, Devonshire Square, was broken into, and a nutnber of gold and silver watches, gold rings and gold albert chains stolen' the whole being valued at about £6O. An entrance had been effected by forcing the lock of the back door, and an inner door had been lifted off its hinges. Unfortunately Mr. Carr had left the safe unlocked, with the keys hanging in the door, and the only barrier to their comfortably carrying off their booty was a ferocious Airedale terrier dog, which, however, they appeared to have easily quieted by some means or other as no alarm was made. The police have ascertained that two of the watches have been pawned at two separate establishments in Warrington, and they hope to secure the robbers in the course of a few days. ,

RUNCORN,

At Runcorn Police Court, on Thur,day. before Major Hazlchurst and Mr. I'. Speakman, Wm. Stubbs, of Rock Savage, and Joseph Allman and George,Williarn, Thos. and Alex Jackson, of Frodsham, were charged on re- mand with assaulting P.S. Costello, on Good Friday night. Three of the men were fighting in the Bridge Inn, at Frodsham Bridge, and the sergeant assisted the landlord to eject them. Afterwards the prisoners com- menced to fight with some Runcorn men and the sergeant interfered when they struck him, kicked him, and threw stones at him injuring him severely. His face was much scarred. He shouted for help, but none came and eventually he got away. Later Inspector Cooper and several constables apprehended the prison- ers at their homes.—Mr. Brassey (Chester), defended. The Bench considered the offence a serious one and fined George Jackson and Joseph Allman £2 and costs 14s. id. each ;and Wm. Stubbs and Thos. and Alexander Jackson 30s. and 14s. Id. each. Wm. Jackson was discharged. If you wantyour watch repaired by skilful workraea. take it to PAITCZAJWI3.-4dVt.

At the conclusion a the football match between St. Helens Recreation and Leeds Parish Church, at t. Helens, on Saturday, and while the enclosure was being cleared, a mason named William Hurst, aged forty-three, who resided with his family at 52, Chorley-street, St. Helens, commenced to vomit blood, and died a few minutes afterwards in the pavilion, to which he had been carried. Deceased had suffered from bronchitis, but was apparently in good health, and had worked that day. . .

PROSPECTUS.

- We would refer our readers to our advertising columns for particulars in respect to the formation of anew company under the tale of The Harvey's Oyster Company, Limited. The object of the com- pany briefly stated, is to purchase two old-estab- lished oyster businesses of Messrs. Harvey and Fearon, oyster dealers, who have oyster beds at Bangor, which possess sufficient space for planting ten million oysters, and licensed establishments in Birmingham, Cheltenham. Derby, Douglas, Dudley. New Brighton, Walsall, Warrington, and Wolver- hampton. The capital of the company is to be £30,000, in shares of £1 each. Messrs. Harvey and F,aron guarantee that t Inn. sales average over three million oysters annually. Mr. Harvey has agreed to guarantee a dividend at the rate of seven per cent, per annum far five years, during which time he is to continue managing director of the company The price fixed for the purchase of the joint pro- perties is £lB,OOO. The company also propose to • purchase three oyster dredgers for use in the Irish Sea and Bristol Channel, and also open up further branches in London, Liverpool, Manchester and other places. Both businesses hitherto have been very lucrative, and been in their respective families many years, and with extended operations will no doubt yield the shareholders a satisfactory divi- dend.

SUPPOSED DEATH FROM EATING SWEETMEATS AT WIDNES. On Saturday afternoon Edward Whelan, aged ten years, son of Patrick Whelan,cheinical labourer,3 North. et., ate a quantity of buns, oranges, and toffee, and at. night in bed he became very sick. His condition did not improve next day, and his father gave him some whisky punch with no good effect and the boy died on Monday morning. An inquest was held by Mr. Brighouse on Wednesday and a verdict of "Natural causes" returned.

At Widnes Police Court, on Monday, before Mr. Deacon, Michael O'Hara, a middle-aged man, who said he was a seaman, was charged with being a wandering lunatic. P.C. Knox said on Sunday night about eleven o'clock he found the prisoner clutching hold of a rail in Ditton-road and acting in a strange manner. The officer asked him what lie was doing and he replied, "Driving a horse and trap through the hedge." P.S.Strickland and Constables Evans and Thompson spoke to the prisoner's I strange conduct in the cells, and the magistrates corn- witted the prisoner to the Workhouse for 14 days. •

EXTRAORDINARY DEATH OF A CHILD AT WIDNES. On Wednesday, at Widnes Polize Court, Mr. S. Brig- house, county coroner, held an inquiry into the death of an infant,five months old, named George William Evans, son of William Evans, engine driver, of 21, Thomas-st.— Sarah Ellen Evans, the mother, deposed that at the re- quest of Mrs. Wilkinson, a monthly nurse, she allowed her child to be taken to the house of Councillor. Single- ton, Victoria-road, to suckle the breasts of Mrs. Single- ton, who had recently given birth to a child. When the Child was put to the brent, Mrs. Singleton said it was quite " cosy " and had gone to sleep, but when the mother went to take the child away rive minutes later it was quite dead. The child had not had a moment's illness since it was born.—Elizabeth Moysey, mother of Mrs. Singleton. gave corroborative evidence.—Mrs. Wil- kinson, the nurse, in reply to the Coroner, said she had adopted the same course previously, in order to relieve a mother of her milk. She did not think at the time that if a doctor had been sent for, Mrs. Singleton could have ' been relieved of her milk by means of a pump. No force was used in putting the child to the breast.—The Coroner said it appeared to him that the breast being hard, the child had been suffocated ny having its face pressed against it.—The jury returned a verdict of "death by misadventure."

THE FORTHCOMING GILCHRIST LECTU RES AT RUNCORN.

A meeting of the committee having in hand the formation of a Town's Committee to carry out the arrangements for this course of interesting lectures. held a meeting on Thursday afternoon,and adjourned for a week. Mr. J. D. Jones, the secretary, was enabled to present a programme of the lectures, which is as follows :—Sir Robert Ball, " Other worlds " ; Mr. A. P. Laurie, "Waves of water and waves of light " ; Professor Marshall, " Pedigrees " ; Dr. Andrew Wilson, "The heart and the story of the circulation " ; Dr. Dellinger, "Spiders, their worth and wisdom " ; Professor Seeley, "Volcanoes." The lectures will begin on October sth and be continued fortnightly until the 14th December. The lecturers are gentlemen of eminence in the scientific world, and will invest their several subjects with entranc- ing interest

RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD.

The monthly meeting was held on Tuesday night Mr. S. Taylor presided, and there were present: the Rev. Dr. Gastaldi, Messrs. E. unison, T. Handley, G. Moss (clerk).—The School Attendance Com- mittee reported that during the month 62 parents had been summoned befare them and 56 had appeared. Proceedings were taken before the magistrates in two cases. On the motion of Mr. Handley, seconded by Dr. Gastaldi a precept for £450 I was issued upon the overseers.—With regard to the proposed temporary school in Bethsada schoolroom the Clerk said he had received a reply from the Education Department. The communication stating that Her Majesty's Inspector for the district had inspected the building and found it fairly satisfactory for the purpose proposed. A form to be filled up was enclosed.—Mr. Handley said it would appear that the Department would allow the Board to take the school if they thought advisable. There was a want of accammodation for girls, according to the returns.—Dr. Gastaldi said the establishmen:: of a new school would entail great ex- pense upon the ratepayers, and there was only a small number of girls for whom it was said there was no accommodation. It simply meant that the schools were a little overcrowded. It was very probable that the accommodation would not be required when the , Ship Canal was finished.—Mr. Handley moved and Mr. Imison seconded that the subject be referred to a com- mittee of the whole Board--Carried.—The Clerk said there had been the highest average attendance during I March than there had ever been previously. The num- ber on the rolls was 138 more than last year, and the ' average attendance 120 more than last year.

'HIGHWAYS AND BUILDINGS COMMITTEE.

THE vicalcutin BRIDGE.

The Mersey Bridge Cousimittee reported the result of the meeting of the Committee of the two County Councils with regard to the proposed vehicular bridge, and stated that in view of the small rateable value of the district, the Commitee could not sem its way to re- commend the Board to increase the offer of £30,003 made on the 4th Nov. last. The chairman, in moving the adoption of the minutes, said although this com- mittee could not see their way to increase the contribu- tion which they thought was sufficient, they did not give up the Droeeciition of this work of obtaining a vehicular bridge across the river. The Widnee Corpora- tion had the matter before them, and their decision would be made knowe at their next meeting. The Com- mittee were of opinion that when the Parish and Dis- trict Councills Bill was passed that it would help for- ward the project. For instance, the Rural Sanitary Authority were informed that they coulal not contribute towards the undertaking, bat if that Bill became law, that authority would have control of the highways, and could do se. He thoueht they could afford to wait a little longer. The might incidentally mention that he had received the followieg letter :—Cuitom House, Runcorn, fith April, 1393. Dear Mr. Wiswall.—Perhaps the Com- missioners may be pleased so hear that the port of Run- corn has been raised from the 2nd to the first class superintendent, from the 19th January last. Tarn also pleased to inform you that I have been promoted accord- inglv,—Youi s faithfully, S. H. Hamilton, superintend- ent?' That was geed news. FRODSHAII

WAGGONETTE ACCIDENT.—ShortIy before nine o'clock on Monday night an accident of a serious nature occurred in Chapel-lane, Frodsham. A waggonette belonging to Mr. Jackson, of Runcorn, driven by Joseph Peacock, Greenwav-road, was coming towards Runcorn, when it came in contact with another vehicle which was passing, the occupants being thrown upon the footpath. Several of them were seriously injured, receiving cuts and bruises about the head, face, and body, also being severely shaken. A baby, twelve months old, had a very narrow escape.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT WARRINGTON.

-- Is it true that there are persons in Warrington who are attempting to take advantage of the in- tention of the Corporation to widen some of the streets in the town, by purchasing property at much over its value? I have heard it said that this is being done, and evidently with the inten- tion of making a hands-me profit out of their 'transactions with the Corporation. It is not im- probable, however, that these rather sharp indi- viduals may get " their fingers burned." Only a short time ago I came across a case of arbitratioa where the sum awarded was considerubly less than had been paid by the persons who had bought the property to mako a profit out of the Local Board. The judge agreed that the price claimed was un- fair.

The numerous friends in Warrington of Mr. W. H Butler, who, a few months ago left this town for America, will be interested in the following extract which is taken from the" Zion Herald" of Boston, of larch 15th. The extract is headed "New England Southern Conference" and reads as follows —" Rev. W. H. Butler, of the English Wesleyan Church, has been appointed by presiding Elder Bar- low preacher in charge of the Wansbeck Church. His participation in the discussions of the Provi- dence preacher's meeting chew him to be a scholarly man and a ready debater." It was, I may remind my readers, the intention of Mr. Butler to join the ministry of the Episcopal Methodist Church of America, and it will be noticed that he has already become entitled to the prefix of " Rev." to his name.

The Rector has now finished his course of "instruc- tions " on the various sects, and the followers of these sects should be considerably enlightened thereby. But why has he left other sects alone in their gross darkness? What have the Presbyteri- ans or the Salvation Army done, that they are not " instructed? " Are they perfect ?or are they toe insignificant for the Rector? The sects, however, which have been "instructed " have not taken that instructicn as they ought to take it from the leader in Warrington of the church, which has such high pretentions. In fact they decline to be con- vinced. Some of them have actually dared to con- trauict him,and,to tell the truth, the Rector appears in some cases to have put up a dummy on a stump to knock it down again. The Rector may also think himself very fortunate if he escapes trou— ble front his own fold, as ,his instruction on the Holy Catholic Church was in reality a defence of the High Church, and what will the Low Church think of it? Would it not be better for the Rector before reproaching Nonconformists of their separa- tion from the Church, to try to settle all divisions within the Church ? as the High and Low Church- men seem, in some cases, more opposed to each other than they are to the common foe.

The annual scene at the St. Barnabas vestry meet- ing took place on Wednesday morning. There was the usual large muster of parishioners, and there were probably more this year than ever, as for some days previous, there had been rumours that there, would be a " lively time." The chief sensation this year, was the defection Mr. Woollacot, the schoolmaster from the vicar, as last year he was his chief supporter, and for this, the vicar taunted him. for his ingratitude, after he (the vicar) had stood by hita during four trials. One of the vicar's party professed to be highly indignant that any but cnurchmen should have anything to do .vita. the election of officers for the church, but this was. effectually rebutted by the answer " Get aisestab•-• lishment then." The two chief opponents sat at. each end of a small table, so that if a resort to. physical arguments had become necessary, they could have been "at " each other without much difficulty. The vestry clerk, Mr. JenkiEs, as usual, was very cool, and declined to commit himself, although he appeared to please the popular sect, as he was overwhelmed at the close of the meeting, with expressions of esteem and regard. I hear that some of the parishioners had teen canvassed to support the vicar for some days before the meeting. but if it is 60, very poor success awaited the efforts of the canvassers as only six persons voted for Mr. Smith's opponent for the eh urchwardenship. The vicar played into the hands of hisopponents by his " too much speaking." Had he been less garrulous, the meeting would probably have been less stormy,. although, no doubt, it was hard to keep quiet when so many charges were being flung about. The meeting which begun and fsr the most part con- tinued like a lion, finished like a lamb. A remark by the vicar, caused a roar of laughter, and this put everyone in a good humour, and from now to the end, there were mutual expressions of good will, and a desire to put an end to an unseemly quarrel and the moeting closed contrary to all expectation, and the sidesmen were elected with scarcely any discussion.

Newton was not troubled very largely with tors this time. Probably the news had gone forth that there was nothing at the end of the visit,more than they could obtain at home. But why tiOnle at- traction is not held out for visitors, I cannot under- stand. There are plenty of tradespeonle who would only be too glad to see an influx of visitors now and again, and would have the greatest pleasure in ac- commodating them with all they required. Of course the chief attraction is the lake, but what is the use of the lake with only two seaworthy boats. The lake is surrounded with Conservatism, and it would perhaps be distinctly unwise to allow visi- tors to flock to the lake and enjoy a sail as in the good old days, when "Sailor Jack "held sway. The working Mall has no need of a holiday, and a little , recreation, away from the grime and smoke of the town. Not he. Ile should stay at home and rwati- cate amongst the chimney pot, if he wants fresh air. Anyway he must not come to NewmtoEnnfoirN.it.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

NOT A SOMNAMBULIST.

Martin O'Hara, a sailor, was charged at the Warring- ton Police Court, on Saturday, with wandering about, and being of unsound mind. He was found opposite the Cemetery Inn, with a crowd round him and acting in a strange mai:men When brought to the police station and questioned he gave very strange answers. It was thought advisable to lock him up. His incarceration seemed to have worked 4 change in his speech for he was able to give the Bench a better account of hirrself. He said he left his ship at Liverpool on Friday, and freely imbibing of beer he went for a constitutional. His little walk extended to Padgate-lane, a distance of over twenty miles, and had not P.C. Clough brought him to the police station he would probably have pro- longed it. In answer to the Bench he said the drink must have affected him, for he did not know when he awoke, but that he was in Liverpool. He felt tired that morning. The magistrates remanded him in order that the medial officer might examine him.

Bi THOUnHT HR WAS IN ARDWICIC. Edward Fell was charged on Thursday at the Warring- ton Police Court, was wandering abroad on Wednesday night apparently of unsound mind. P.C. Clough said that be was called into the Wagon and Horses public house in Buttermarket-street on the previous night as prisoner was in that place and was very strange in manner and made very rambling statements. Ha said that he was in Ardwick, Manchester, and had walked from Liverpool. Witness, however, learnt that he had been lodging at Gallops during the night. He could get no sensible statement out of him and he Was brought to the police station. Inspector Spinks said that when in the police station, prisoner said that he had been at Ohmy's Circus at Crewe. A letter was found from his wife in Ashton-under-Lyne but he begged witness not to write to her. Dr. Cannell had examined him and pro- nounced him to be unfit to be at large at present. Prisoner was ordered to be handed over to the relieving officer.

ASSAULT CASES.

At the Warrington-l—ogh---CoUrt on Friday, Annie Howard, 57 Chorley-st., was charged with assaulting aad beating Martha Jones, 59 Chorley-st., on the 28th ult. Martha Jones said that as defendant was calling her daughter names, she went to take her daughter's part, when defendant struck her. Defendant was fined 7e. 6d. including costs. Samuel Ball, 24 Dial court, was charged by Clara Lythgoe, 1 Dial court, with commit- ting a similar offence on April 3rd. Complainant said that last Monday,defendant came and knocked her down in her own house. She afterwards had a fight with Miss Kenny,viho keeps company with defendant, and defend- ant came to take his young lady's part, and seized complainant by the throat,and also gaveher a black eye Several witnesses among whom were Miss Kenny, were called to prove that the black eye was caused by the fight with Miss Kenny, and the magistrates dismissed the case ordering each to pay their own costs. George Henry Hollier, 100, Cartwright-street, was charged with assaulting his wife on April 3rd. Mrs. Hollier said that her husband had had a little drink on Monday. When they were going to bed he struck her in the eye, making it black. She wished for a separation. Defendant said that he was vexed at having to wait out- side, as his wife was away and had the key. He was very sorry for what he had done, and promised not to do it again. The magistrates dismissed the case, and hoped the two parties would live together again com- fortably. The wife, however, on leaving the court, said she would not live with him again.

RUGBY. WARRINGTON y. LEIGH.

There was the usual monotony of forward play at- tached to the first meeting of the above clubs, which rendered the match anything but interesting. For fully sixty minutes out of the seventy played the three- quarters on both sides were only seen on defensive tactics, the bulk of the aggressive work being done by the forwards. The match took place at Leigh, and there was a gate of nearly 3,000 at the kick-off. The day turned out wet, rain falling throughout the game. Warrington turned out as advertised, whilst Leigh had also their strongest team on. Some twenty minutes after the time advertised to kick off

Cook Started, and Heesom was the first to show up with a long punt which found touch at half-way. Scrimmaging was the order until a free kick to Leigh found them trespassing on the visitors' 25. Again Heesom relieved, and this time being ably backed up by Higham and Jolley, Coop was passed before he clould clear. The Leigh back, how- ever,recovered his reputation a minute later, but Boscow duly responded,only to see Anderton return,and Heesom's return being a trifle weak the homesters attacked in strong force,ably led by Anderton and France. Packs were formed under the Warrington posts, and the visitors screwing a scrimmage finely, Jolley came away from the ruck and gathering well put a finish to his effort by kicking toCoop, who placed into touch at his quarter- flag. Exchanges between Boscow and Anderton saw the Warrington custodian very safe, but an eager at- tention towards the Leighite lost a matter of thirty yards. Leigh now displayed some plucky passing, but the Bates for the first time showed up, and play again settled down at half-way. Another spell of rough-and- ' tumble forward play eventually ended in Leigh rushing down the open to Boscow, who met the opposing force finely, and, dodging a couple of opponents, he cleared the danger in fine style. Ashton, Fair Turner, and , Donohue were the leaders in another rush, whilst a smart follow-up by Berry almost caught Prescott nap- ping. Again the forwards monopolized attention, and ten minutes were spent on the popular side in scrimmag- ing and line-outs. Eventually the Warrington half backs got possession, and James Bate, dodging round i the pack, ran finely, and throwing across to Heesom, the latter was knocked into touch near the line. Turner, Jolley, and Nevins were to the fore in another rush, but Lawless having a kick charged Evans stepped in the breach and saved well at half-way. From a line-out Fair Turner took the ball down the open, and charging a kick by Prescott, it looked a certainty that a score would take place, when the Warringtonians over-ran the ball, and a capital chance was thus, lost. The visitors were now having. decidedly the best of it, and after Berry had responded to a run by Evans, Bos- cow with a grand screw kick landed the oval once more on the home lines. Ewan got away from a scrimmage on the line, but fell into the grasp of Jolley, and need- less to add he did not travel very far. A mark to Bosomy saw Fair. Turner having a shot at goal, but only a minor accrued. Frran the drop-out Frank Turner raced down to Coop, and tackled his man on his lines. The next scrimmage saw James Bate defying opposition, and after this he had the ill-luck to ground the wrong side of the line. This was followed by an exciting incident, for John Bate threw across to Heesom, and the latter preferring a dr.ap at goal, the shot fell short, but Evans racing up, fielded the oval finely, and ran in between the posts. To the surprise of the Warrington- ians, the point was over-ruled, and only a minor allowed. From the drop-out James Bate made his mark, and Turner had another shot at goal, sending up the third minor. Evans next charged a kick of South's, and relief came to Leigh when half-time was announced with the score of three minors to War- rington. Fair. Turner re-started, and Ashton led the way to the home quarters, whilst both Bates supple- mented this by shot kicks, which took the game within easy reach of the Leigh goal. Ewan was grassed by John Bate before he could clear, but Coop cleared splen- didly, Boscow's reponse being a trifle weak. The latter, however, redeemed himself a minute later, for, being surrounded by a host of opponents, he cleared grandly, and a lofty Mint saw the Warring,tonians once morekon the aggressive. South took the oval to half-way, but the visitors would not be denied, and another fine bit of play by the Leigh forwards was seconded by Bouth, and when it looked?! big odds on the homesters getting in, Heesom raced in front, and saved In daring style.

WARRINGTON V. ROCHDALE ST. CLEMENT'S. This return match was played at Wilderspool on Easter Monday. RUNCORN v. Sr. HELENS.—PIayed at Runcorn, in grand weather, on Monday before a tremendous gate. Cross started,and Crompton returned to Dougherty,who kicked into touch. The play was fast. The St. Helens team st *wed well for a time Dougherty and Sudlow doing good work, and for a time Runcorn were a bit pressed, but a forward rush by them took play into the middle of the ground, and Anderton, of Leigh, who played cen- tre for St. Helens tackled, Warder grandly prevented a ' return. The latter player, however, kicked finely, the ball rolling behind the line. For a long time the play was very hard, both teams doing their utmost, and Cromp- ton ran the ball up to the St. Helens line, but, slipping, missed the opportunity to score. Play continued very fast, there being very little between the teams. On the verge of half-time, which arrived with a clean sheet, Runcorn had very hard lines. After the interval, the same keen determined play continued a very long time. Each side attacked in turn. Crompton ran and kicked to the line ; but Anderton soon got the ball away, Houghton replying splendidly. Still nothing was scored, and play was very hard, and a more even game was• never fought out at Runcorn, only minors being scored.

TIIEBROOII v. WIDNES.—The venue of this match was changed from Widnes to Tuebrook, but only a poor gate turned up to witness operations, no doubt the rain which was coming down at the start deterring many from putting in an appearance. Half time arrived with Widnes leading by 1 try to nil. On crossing over, the game was of a ding-dong character, and was stubbornly contested all through the second half. At one period the homesters looked as if they were going to equalise. same of the Widnes men being over the line in the "scrim," but when the ball was once loose the visitors put the result beyond doubt as by some judicious passing they gradually forged their way to the other end, where Gordon secured a try, and Drummond converted from an easy place. Final result :—Widnes lgoal 1 try,Tue- brook, nil.

RUNCORN v. GARSTON L. AND N.-W. RECREATION.— Played on the ground of the former, before a fair num- ber taf spectators, considering the state of the weather. Runcorn were without Pye, Kidd, Wright, and Paterson, while Garston came one short. Runcorn won the toss, and todk advantage of the wind. Garston kicked off, and the game became pretty even until Arthur Limb, about ten minutes from the start, sent in at the far

corner a lovely shot, thus drawing first blood for Run- corn. Then Runcorn continued to press for some time until Garston broke away, and from a rush by their centre and nght wing forwards carried the ball past Mainwaring,thus equalizing. Even play continued until half-time was reached, with the score—Runcorn, one goal ; Garston, one. On resuming Runcorn, in spite of their playing against the wind,had somewhat the best of the game, and experienced rather hard lines in not scor- ing, several shots being put in, and on one occasioa 'lampoon, from a pass into the centre, healed nicely, though the ball went just outside the upright. Untrue kicking by the Runcorn backs let in the visitors several times, giving comers, but orce the ball, slipping off the toe of one of the backs,was sent tewardsthe goal mouth, and Mainwaring missing his kick, an easy goal resulted for Garston. Still Runcorn pressed, but with all their efforts they were unable to equalize,and the game ended in a victory for Garston by two goals to one.

The annual Easter vestry meeting was held in the vestry of Christ Church, Croft, on Tuesday forenoon, when there was only a small attendance, including the Rector, the Rev. H. 1. Wadeson, M.A. (who presided), and Messrs. W. Priestner and R. Turner (churchwardens) R. Owen, sen., T. Southern, A. Dixon, and D. Waywell (sexton). Southern were elected sides- men. Mr. Southern introduced the subject of raising the seats in the centre of the church, and announced his intention of giving a substantial donation towards the work. HOLY TRINITY CHURCH. The vestry meeting of the Holy Trinity Church, was held on Tuesday night, the Vicar (the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton), in the chair. There were present Messrs. G. F. F. White, J. Hesketh, Rowson, Harrison, Hoult, McGregor, Hatton, Welsh, Dunbabbio, Harding, James Fairclough, and Jefferson. Mr. McGregor read the financial statement which showed that the income for the yeaewas £273. Owing to the extra expenditure, caused by the pointing of the church, there was an adverse balance of £l5. The vicar considered this to be a very creditable state of affairs when the large expendi- ture in the alterations of the church could be borne with- out any special appeal to the public. He regretted that Mr. McGregor did not wish to be his warden during the coming year,as he had done good work during the past, he would therefore nominate Mr. Welsby as his warden. Mr. James Fairclough was unanimously elected people's warden. The following sidesmen were elected Messrs. J. Hatton, R. W. Rowson, John Halton, Edwin Gaskell, U. Floult, J. Harding, E. Booth, Dunbabin, John White E. Rose, J. flesketh, J M. Beacon, W. J. Forshaw, H. J. Westbrook, Peers, senr., Pickstone, Flollev, and Warbur- ton. White, vestry clerk; and E. A. White and Harrison, auditors. A vote of thanks was accorded to the retiring Wardens, the organist and the choir, for their services during the past year. ST. JAMES CHURCH, LATCHFORD : A COMPLAINT

OF SMALL OFFERTORIES.

St. James Church annual vestry meeting was held in the vestry, on Monday morning, when there were pre- sent the Rev. F. Slater (in the chair), Mr. C. Sawkins Burgess, (vicar's warden), Mr. P. Hutchinson, J. Mill- wood, Mr. J. H. Warburton, (sidesmen) ; and Mr. Stansfield. The accounts for the year showed receipts including ninepence in hand from last year amounting to £234 is. 10d., and the expenditure including a sum of £l2 in hand amounted to £234 Is. 10d. In reading these accounts Mr. Burgess said he felt honoured at be- ing selected as Vicar's warden for so many years, but he had accepted office this year with some amount of trepidation. The Church would require to be very much repaired both externally and internally, and there was little prospect of securing the wherewithal to carry out this. He also complained of the small offertories which were taken in the church and said that out of the total of £234 they had obtained £7O from outside. The collections that year had been somewhat better than in other years.—The Vicar nominated Mr. Burgess as his warden for the ensuing year, and then read a communi- cation from Mr.Tauranac,people's warden,who had gone to reside in London. The writer regretted that he had to sever his connection with St. James', and said he should have been nleased to attend the meeting if possi- ble, but added " I shall be otherwise engaged, however, and so shall think of you. About the time your meeting is being held I shall be leading a bride to the altar." (Hear, hear.)—Mr. Warburton was appointed warden in the stead of Mr. Tauranac. Messrs. Venn, Jackson, Shaw, Cotton, Millwood and Stansfield, were appointed sidesmen, and Messrs. Houghton, and Burgess lay representatives to the ruri-deaconalconference.

The Chairman in moving a vote of thanks to the wardens for their work during the past year, said that in connection with the church they had had a year of difficulty in many ways, but it had been a year of wonderful encourage- ment, whether in the work of their ehurch, or in the schools they could see reasons for very great encourage- ment, and the difficulties which confronted them twelve months ago had been overcome one by one, as they had had to meet them. The resolution was carried unani- mously. The chairman then nominated Mr. Page as his warden for the ensuing year. Mr. Lees was appointed people's warden, on the proposition of Mr. Allen, second- ed by Mr. Gandy. Mr. Page nominated Mr. Norman as his sidesinan, and Mr. Lees nominated Mr. Henry Gandy, Mr. A. Dickens, and Mr. VI. Atherton, were also elected sidesmen. The following were elected vestrymen.:. Messrs. H. Gandy, W. Parsons, F. Gandy. J. Allen, E. Allen, W. Atherton, and J. Ashbrook. Votes of thanks to the organists, secretary, and the chairman concluded the meeting.

ST. ANNE'S CHURCH: "A DEAD LOCK."

The vestry meeting in connection with St. Anne's Church was held on Tuesday morning. The notice announcing the meeting stated that the business of the vestry would be commenced at 10.30, but the Vicar (the Rev. J. G. Richards) did not take the chair until eleven o'clock. There were then also present Messrs. H. Wood (vestry clerk), T. Rigby, J. Baird, James Baird Gibbon, Friar, Bagnall, Bandies, and F. Taylor. Mr. Woods having read the minutes of the last meeting, announced that the next business would be the appoint- ment of a vicar's warden. The Vicar : I nominate Mr. Peter Aldred as my warden. Mr. Woods : The next business is the appointment of peoples' warden.—The Vicar (after a considerable pause) : I beg to propose Mr. Rigby.—Mr. !Rigby : I decline to accept any office in connection: with the church.—The Vicar : Will you accept the office Mr.

Baird.—No sir, I cannot see my way to accept any office.—The Vicar : Mr. James Baird, will you take the office.—No sir, I must decline.—The Vicar : This is a nice predicament. We are at a dead lock. Mr. Friar won't you take the office of warden.—No sir, I don't care to.—The Vicar : Why P—Well, I'm not a parish- ioner.—The Vicar : You are.—No. I am not sir. I am in lodgings and have not got a vote.—The Vicar : But that doesn't matter.—lt does to me, sir. I rPfuse to take any office from private reasons.—The Vicar then asked the Vestry Clerk what would have to be done under the circumstances, and the Clerk replied that they could only enter a minute to the effect that there was no qualified parishioner present who would accept the office.

The next business was the appointment of sidesmen. The vicar appealed to the whole of the meeting individu- ally but met with the same answers—no one would accept office.—The Vicar: Dere you are again ; at a per- fect deadlock. Can't we postpone the meetiry, Mr. Woods.—No sir. Our next business is the appointment of lay representatives to the ruri-deaconal conference.— The Vicar : Am I privileged to nominate anyone.—Yes, you area parishioner.—The Vicar : Then I propose Mr. Aldred.—Mr. Dagnall : I will second the motion.—The Vicar. That's it Dagnall you are becoming useful. The proposition was then put to the meeting hut no one seemed to care be vote either for or against the appoint- ment of Mr. Aldred.—The Vicar : What are we to do when a meeting absolutely refuses to vote Mr. Woods ? I don't know.—The Vicar: Its a pity we havn't got the power at a vestry now that we used to have in the olden times. We are at a perfect deadlock.

At this juncture, Mr. Stringer left the room. The Vicar asked where he could be going to.—Mr. Rigby (looking at his watch) : He is going to business I expect. I have come here at great inconvenience—in the middle of the morning, and we were kept waiting outside for half an hour.—The Vicar : Is there any other business of the parish to be transacted P—Mr.Wood :—No.—The Vicar : Chen there's nothing more for the reporters P—No.—A Reporter : I presume you require the press to leave the room .—The Vicar : Yes.—The reporters then retired.

_ always put it.—Mr. R. r. Smith: Is it not a customary thing that the church wardens shculd sign the notice convening the meeting ? Mr. Jenkins; It does not mat- ter at all. It is perfectly regular that the notice con- vening the meeting should be signed by the vicar alone. It is quite a matter of taste.

AN INCONVENIENT TIME.

Mr. Benjamin (to the vicar) :Bow is it that you should call the meeting at such an inconvenient hour for the parishioners ?—Mr. Battersby : I intended to bring that forwaro. I think it wrong to summon a body of workingmen at such an untimely hour. (Hear, hear.) If we must attend here, we neglect something else. I think the officers of the church should arrange matters for the convenience of the majority of the parishioners. (Hear, hear.) I move that this meeting stand adjourn- ed until to-night. Mr. Jenkins : You are out of order. The business of this meeting is to elect two churchwar- dens and four sidesmen. When you have disposed of that business, you can discuss other matters.—Mr. Proud :Is Mr. Jenkins parish clerk ? He was not appointed last year.—Mr. M. T. Smith : As an officer of this church, I do say that a church vestry meeting ought to be appointed at a convenient time for the working- class to have a fair opportunity to attend as ratepayers of this parish. Mr. Battersby : I have moved the motion and shall persist in it.—The Vicar : You are out of order. I call—Mr. Battersby : I again move that this meeting be adjourned until seven o'clock.—The Vicar: We always have our meetings in the morning in this town.—Mr. Benjamin : There was one last night.—The Vicar : This must always be in the morning,—Mr. Jenkins : It would be hard to please everybody, although I think an even- ing meeting to be the most sensible. (Hear, hear.)— Mr. Bat :ersby : If we have power to transact business, we have power to adjourn.—Mr. Princep : I don't think it is fair to the clergy and to members of the Church of England, that anyone should have a voice in the election of officers.—Mr. Benjamin : Get disestablished then ! Mr. Smith : The meeting should be constituted of rate- payers, whether church or chapel, independent or sect altogether.—Mr. Battersby: I withdraw my resolution, hoping that it will be considered in the future.—Mr. Jenkins then read the minutes of the last meeting. Mr. Smith : I was appointed a lay representative last year without my knowledge. It was not brought for- ward at the meeting, and is not recorded in the minutes. How is that ?—Mr. Jenkins : Because it was not men- tioned at the meeting.

THE CDURaWARDENS: THEIR DUTY.

The Vicar : The next business is to elect two church- wardens for the parish, who will fulfil their duties in the parish of this church. If anyone will make a nomination I shall be pleased.—Mr. Princep : I have a gentleman to propose, but before I make that proposition I should like to ask what are the duties of the churchwardens, and have the late churchwardens done their duty according te ecclesiastical law P—Mr. Jenkins : I would recom- mend you to buy a book on the subject, it is only 7s. 6d. It is too much to read at this meeting. (Laughter.)— Mr.Princep : I understand the late churchwardens have not done the duties of their office. They have not re- ceived any subscriptions, and they have paid no bills whatever. Has Mr. Smith paid any bills during the last twelve months P—Mr. Smith : I will answer that ques- tion. I was duly appointed churchwarden of this parish last vestry meeting. Mr. A. Byrom, the Vicar's warden, did not subscribe to the declaration required, and the money collected in the parish was deposited in the safe until such time as my co-warden subscribed to the declara- tion. In the meantime, the Vicar paid several items, independent of the churchwardens. He never brought a single account before me. and never consulted me about a single pas ment.—The Vicar : I may say that I paid the gas bill, as I received a notice (which the Vicar read) that the gas would be cut off, if the churchwardens did not find the money. I have other letters which I

might read, but you are not my congrention. I shall bring them before a church meeting. 1. have got re, ceipts for all the money I have spent. I may tell you in all kindness, that as long as Mr. Smith is in ofilt,T, quar- relling with the Vicar, there will be no good done in this church. lam determined that I will never again keep another account. If the account is on the vestry floor I will not pick it up. During the time I have had the management of the church I have kept it entirely tree from debt, and we have a balance of 9s.—Mr. Benjamin : Has the account come in for the paving of the street be- fore the school ?—The Vicar : I cannot say. Will some one propose a people's warden ?—Mr. Smith: Will you nominate your warden P—The Vicar declined until the people's warden was elected.—Mr. Princep proposed Mr. Wm. Burton, and Mr. Win Hill seconded.—Mr. Lewis Eden proposed, and Mr. Bailey seconded the nomination of Mr. R. P. Smith. THE STATE 07 THE SCHOOLS : BOARD SCHOOL SUGGESTED. The Vicar was about to put the two names, when Mr. CVoollacot said he desired to support the nomination of Mr.R.T.Smith as a churchwarden,because by that office he would be manager of the day school. It was a most impor- tant thing to get right and proper men as wardens, in order that the school might be properly managed. The day schools had been very much mismanaged. There should be five managers of the school, but the Vicar had taken upon himself to act without them. The Vicar dis- missed teachers, not paying their salaries for months 'together, and there had been no fire in the school since Christmas.—The Vicar : That is a, school question.— Mr. Woollacot : But it is one of the most important duties of the wardens of the parish.—Mr. Jenkins sup- ported Mr. Woollacot's contention.—Mr. Woollacot, con- tinuing, said form 9 was sent down by the department to be filled up. It was filled up by himself, and ought to have been filled up by the Vicar. The vicar called a meeting to his house about it, at the time when there was illness at his house. They were very sorry for that illness, but was that a proper place for a school manager's meeting ? The managers of course refused to attend. The vicar was called to a meeting at the school three times, but would not come, and sent Mr. Smith a very insolent reply. Was that a proper way to meet a church- warden ? (" No ! ne !") Some of the teachers were starving. He had not had any money himself for a long time. (Shame.)—The Vicar in reply alluded to the fact that he had stood by Mr. Woollacot in four trials, and said that the blame for the delay in the filling up of form 9 rested on the shoulders of Mr. Woollacot. It would be necessary to have a school board„if the managers could not conduct the present school, but he had not asked the Town Clerk to form one.—Mr. Smith : You should allow the managers to conduct it. You act as if you were everybody.—The Vicar: It is you Mr. Smith who does that.—Mr. Smith : You should treat your managers fairly and do your busines-, in a straight- forward manner.—The Vicar : The solicitor has advised me to do as I have done. I should be thankful to get out of the" job "of the schools. I. want to have noth- ing at all to do with them, but I cannot get out of them as they are church property.—Mr. Eden said his children went to the school and complained of there being no fire.—Mr. Haley asked if the vicar would not promise to co-operate with the majority of the managers in the coming year so as to create a better feeling.—The Vicar Yes, I will in everything that is legal.—The names of Mr. R. T. Smith and Mr. William Burton were then put to the meeting, and Mr. &pith was elected by an over- whelming majority, only six voting for Mr. Burton. The Vicar appointed Mr. Thomas Henry Griffiths-as his war- den.—Mr. Battersby asked if Mr. Griffiths was not a singer in the Unitarian Chapel.—The Vicar : He is a churchman, and I am glad to get him to work with Mr. Smith.

Messrs. William Burton, William Throssall, William Fairclough, and Mr. Charles Princep were appointed sidesmen.

" ALLS WELL THAT ENDS wntt."

The Vicar in concluding the business said that through- out the past year, in spite of all that had been said of the church, there had been satisfactory progress. A person had presented them with a manual organ har- monium,and the services had been verybright,and another person had punted the outside of the church free of ex- pense. He hoped that those present would assist Mr. Smith and the other officials to help in making the church more prosperous. He wished to come to an agreement with Mr. Smith, and to put an end to these quarrels. He should object to nothing unless it was against his prin- ciples,--Mr. Smith replied, and expressed his thanks to the Vicar for his kind remarks.—Several present also ex- pressed their regard to Mr. Jenkins for the able way in which he had acted as vestry clerk. The proceedings then terminated.

FARNWORTH PARISH CHURCH.

The annual vestry was held iu the chancel of the church on Monday evening. The Rev. J. Wright Wil- liams, vicar, presided and there was a good attendance. The Chairman in opening the proceedings expressed the pleasure he felt in thus meeting the parishioners for the first time in vestry assembled. The warden's accounts read by Mr. .1. L. Hunter, showed total receipts £ll3 15s. Bd., and after paying all expenses there was a balance of £2 13s. ld. A discussion arose as to some Ditton land tax of £1 18s. 6d. per annum, which was four years in arrears and the tenant had now left. The Vicar said he had ascertained that by a deed executed some years, Mr. Brock and Mr. Carey were the trustees of the tax.—Mr. Kidd said they could recover the tax from the landlord if the tenant did not pay.—The Vicar thought if they left the matter in the hands of the trus- tees it would be followed up.—Mr. Brock said he, with his co-trustee, wduld do everything possible to recover the money and to get the tax properly paid in future.— The Vicar also informed the vestry that a small book had come into his possession which had solved the mystery of the origin-of the £l5O invested in consols,and of which the church wardens regularly received the interest from the bank. In 1804 £267 was subscribed for the purchase 1 of an organ for the church. The organ cost £lB4 178.10 d. and the balance of £B2 94. 4W was invested in the three ' per costs. The sum was equal to purchasing £l5O of that stock.—Mr. Thorpe considered that the interest ought to have been allowed to accumulate instead of being devoted towards paying church expenses. They would now have had a good sum towards purchasing the new organ that was needed.—The Vicar pointed out that the interest had been used towards paying the organists salary since 1807.—The subject then dropped. —The Vicar appointed Mr. John Brock. J.P., as his war- den and the meeting appointed Mr. Thomas Fletcher, warden for Bold, Mr. G. I. Neil warden, for Cuerdley, and Mr. W. B. Longton, warden for Cronton.—Messrs. T. Shuttleworth and S. Hoyle were elected lay represen- tatives. The following sidesmen were elected :—Messrs. ' P. Longton, Joseph Glover, Thomas Baker, C. E. Barlow, H. Brown, A. Bradshaw, T. Carlile, J. W. Davies, Thos. Fletcher, E.Guest,S.floyle,J.L.Hunter,W.Jones, R. Kidd, J.R.Len,H.S.Lea,F.S.Lea,W.S. Pritchard ,T.Shuttlewort h, C.Whitfield, A. Kidd, Thomas Warburton, A. Thorpe, T. Knowles, and A. Harrison. Votes of thanks were ac- corded to the wardens, sidesmen, and other officers of the church for their services during the past year.

Linaker, Vicar's warden, replied that it was a duly convened meeting of the parishioners, and he ruled Mr. Foulkes out of order.—Mr. Foulkes: The vicar is chairman of this meeting. I wont be over- ruled by you.—Mr. Linaker : I rise to a point of order. —Mr. Foulkes : Let the vicar attend to it. We had enough of you last year.—Mr. Linaker: You will have a little more to-day perhaps.

WHO WAS CHAIRMAN?

NEW FREE LIBRARY AND TECHNICAL SCHOOL FOR ST. HELENS.

GENEROUS GIFT BY COLONEL GAMBLE, C.B.

At the menthly meeting of the St. Helens Town Council, on Wednesday, Alderman Richard ton the chairman ol the Technical Education Corn- mittee read the following letters : Windlehurst, sth April, 1893. Dear Colonel Pilkington,—The reference to the question of the provision of additional accommo- dation for the technical education classes, to be considered by a sub-committee, which appears in the minutes to be brought before the council to- night, induces me to at once make the offer which I enclose herein. I have for several months in- tended to make this offer, bill have delayed it under the impression that we scarcely yet knew what classes for technical education will be per- manently promoted by the Council.—Yours faith- fully, DAVID GAMBLE.

The following is the letter referred to : Windlehurst, St. Helens, April 5, 1893. To the Chairman of the Technical Education Committee. Dear Sir,-1 beg to offer to the Council of the borougk of St. Helens the piece of vacant land, bounded by Cor- poration-st., Hardshaw-st., and Bickerstaffe-st., St. Helens, which contains 1,510 square yards, exclusive of streets,the leasehold interest in which is for 999 years, I have recently acquired, and which is subject to a rent of £llB 15s. fixed twenty years ago, for the purpose of the erection thereon of a central library and reading-room for the town, and a school for technical education and ' manual instructicn. I hope the area is large enough for both purposes, and I believe that it can be covered by a proper building for the sum of £20,000, which I propose t!' to place in the bank to be expended for the object by a committee, composed of the Mayor, the Chairman of the Technical Education Committee, and the Chairman of the Library Committee, all for the time being, together with three gentlemen to be named by me or my repre- sentatives, of whom I propose to be one for the present. Perhaps it may be necessary to define the purposes for ' which the building is to be used more clearly than I have done above, or than I can do without consultation with the proposed committee ; but it is my intention that it should not be used for a museum nor for ordinary secondary education, nor that a principal use should be for art classes without industrial application.—Yours faithfully, DAVID GAMBLE. (Loud applause followed the reading of the letter). Alderman Pilkington said the communication came upon him as a great surprise, as it had done upon the Council, but he thought the sooner he let them know of it the better. (Hear, hear.) It took his (Alderman Pilkington's) breath away when he was told about izin the Mayor's parlour, and he really did not know what to say. He would, however, say that their best thanks were due to the Colonel for that most noble gift. (Applause.) It was a splendid gift, and one that very few towns of the size of St. Helens received. Councillor Martin said the generous gift of Colonel Gamble would be appreciated by the Library Committee, and he was sure the inhabitants at large would value the gift most deeply, and feet most grateful for it. Councillor Edward Johnson, as deputy-chairman of the Library Committee, heartily endorsed all that had been said. The Library Committee had laboured under difficulties, and the Technical Education Committee having gone forward, it had become more and more evident every day that the Council would have to go to a large expense in providing accommodation. The gift, coming as a surprise, seemed to take their breath away. The Mayor, who is associated with Colonel Gamble,. and has been so for many years,said it was most gratify' ing to him, as Mayor of the borough, to be able to put the resolution to the meeting. (Applause.) The heartiest thanks of the Council were tendered to Colonel Gamble.

Mr. Brighouse, on Tuesday,held inquests at St.Helens. on the bodies of Margaret Ellison, aged fifty-seven, of Ardwick-st.,and Jane Watkinson,aged eighty-two years, of St. Helens Junction, who both sustained fatal injuril by falling downstairs. A verdict of "Accidental death ' was returned in each case, there being no suspicion and no blame attaching to an/one.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held oa Wednesday morning at the Town Hall. There were then present Messrs. R. Heaton (in the chair), W. Balharry, Auckland, Muir, G. Pilling, Wright, Davies, Longton, A. Jolley, A. J. Jolley, Gill, Wm. Fairclough, T. Smith, J. Turton, J. Clarke,- G. Ratcliffe, J. Houghton, R. Owen, T. Mather, Win. Houghton, J. C. Sutton (clerk), Ward (Workhouse master), H. M. Mines (superintendent), Dr. Peacock° (medical officer). THE SUPPLY OF COFFINS. . .

Mr. Dorisox Inq—u—ired if the contract for the supply of coffins would be re-let in consequence of the death of Mr. Lowden Wells.—lt was stated that Mr. Lowden Wells, , would carry on the contract. • VACCINATION OFFICER'S FEES.

• ItIP.RATCLIFFE inquired if the vaccination officer's fees would not be greatly increased in consequence of the recent epidemic. The CLuitx in reading the accounts stated that the number of cases which the vaccination officer had received fees for were 339 in January, 161 in February, and 142 in March. The total was 642 and the sum paid £2l 6s. In addition to this there was £1 ss. sd. paid for postage and 15s. for posters. Mr. R. Owric inquired if the vaccination officer was receiving fees for the re-vac- cination of adults.—The CLERK : I believe that he does not. It is probable that owing to the prevalence of the epidemic a much larger number of children have been vaccinated.—Mr. SHAW: It would be better to know whether there are included cases of re-vaccination.—The anus : The bill has been made out in accordance with the certificates which have been received of successful vaccination. I have not got the bill here se that I can- not say.—Mr. OWEN : If I knew that adults were included I should vote against the passing of the accounts.—Mr. SHAW : I cannot see that all these are primary vaccinations or else the population has been increasing very rapidly. (Laughter.)—Mr. Mina I should like to know whether it is not illegal for the vaccination officer to include the re-vaccination cases in his accounts.—The Clerk sent out for the accounts which upon being produced showed that the who% of the 642 cases were certified primary vaccination. • A PAUPER'S names. The CLERK read a report from the relieving officer concerning the case of a blind man named Garmoyle. from whose wife a letter had been received at the last meeting, complaining that the Union Medical Officer would not attend her husband. The report showed that the man was 70 years of age, and lived at 22, Percivals- fold. For many years he had been receiving 3s. per week outdoor relief from the Union. He was in the habit of going into the Little Weint leading to the mar- ket on Wednesdays and Saturdays, and begging alms. He was taken ill however and removed to the Work • house, but he refused to remain there and had soon resumed his mercenary career. He was taken to the Workhouse again but refused to remain in, and when asked whether he would desist from begging and be •content with relief he said "No," and preferred to beg rather than accept the assistance of the Union. His wife said he realized as much as 2s. per day; begging.— Mr. Szfaw said the man had told the Board that begging was a better "job "than living in the Workhouse.—The CLERK said be would send tile report to the Local Gov- ernment Board.

IN COMFORTABLE QUARTERS. On the motion of Mr. A. JOLLEY, seconded by Mr. Saew, the Board decided to retain the use of the Town Council Chamber for their meetings for another three months.

DR. GORNALL'S CONTRACT. Mr. SHAW said that in accordance with a request he made at the last meeting, be had been supplied with a copy of the contract which Dr. Gornall held as vaccina- tion officer to the Union. From the contract he perceived that Dr. Gornall's contract was a yearly one, but it had not been formally renewed for the past five years. He thought this was not right to the younger medical men of the town. He considered that all contracts of such a character should for public benefit be competed for at the termination of each contract. As the contract could be now ended by a 28 day's notice, he begged to move that such a notice be given to Dr. Gornall to terminate his ccntract as public vaccinator to the Union. He thought it was not right that Dr. Gornall should prevent younger medical men of the town—who were as capable as him—from receiving the sweets of such an office. A discussion of some length took place on the matter, and eventually, on the advice of the Clerk, Mr.Shaw,for the purpose of giving all members of the Board an oppor- tunity to know of his intention, gave notice of a motion that he would bring forward at the next meeting for the purpose of gi.,ing Dr. Gornall notice to terminate his contract in accordance with agreements in the contract. Mr.Sbaw, in the course of his remarks upon the subject, stated that the smallpox epidemic was en the increase in the town.

WARRINGTON SAILING CLUB.

ANNUAL BOAT RACE.

Saturday last was the occasion of the 23rd annual boat race held in connection with the Warrington Sailing Club. The race, however, seems to be los- ing something of its old interest, which is probably the result of the arrangement which exists of intro- ducing no new feature into the affair, and continu- ing the very much condemned system of running the boats round the buoy at the Five Acres and continuing the race--on the ebb—back to Litton's Mill. All the boats left on Good Friday for Snig Pie House, Widnes, where the race commences. Thtre was only one alteration on last year's entries, and that was the entrance of" Mischief "which took the place of" Tom Bowling." The new bcat however, got into mischief on its trip down, and grounded on Widnes bank, opposite Cuerdley Marsh. The various crafts were measured by the keel, and handicapped a minute per foot, " Pink " (captained by W. Warburton) having 12 minutes start Of the scratch boat" Gipsy." Following "Fink " came "May Fly" (Captain, T. Sherratt), " Mischief "(Capt. A. Spencer), ' Primrose (Capt. S. Leigh)," Florence" (Captain, H. Warburton), " Rose" (Capt. W. Warbur- ton), " Bessie" (Capt. James Green), " Woodcock " (Captain J. Peers), "Maggie" (Captain John Suyton) and " Gipsy " (Captain James Davison). The run from Widnes down to Bank Quay was accompanied by a tameness greater than that which has characterized the last two or three races. There was a very pleasant "slip" breeze blowing, not sufficiently strong, however, to bring into play the qualities a any particular boat, unless it was • Maggie." Thi-A boat, which was destined to come in for neither of the first three prizes sailed the best of any in the race, and so far as legitimate sailing went showed "a clean pair of heels" to every boat, and came down to the buoy at the five acres some seconds ahead of "Pink," the boat which was leading the fleet by almost a quarter of a mile down to Bank Quay. When " Pink " was thus beaten by " Maggie " she was closely followed by" Bessie," "May Fly," "Florence," "Rose," "Primrose," "Woodcock," "Gipsy," and "Mischief." Owing to the race having commenced too early on the flood, the whole a the boats were com- pelled to lie practically abreast of each other at the buoy for 20 minutes until the ebb set in. This was almost equivalent to commencing the race from there, back to the Mill, a distance of about 200 yards. The boats were all able to start again at the same time, so that the results of the ten miles sailing from Widnes were in no way taken into account, no extra start being given to the boats which came to the buoy several minutes before the others. This was a sub- ject for adverse criticism on the part of the spectators, who generally considered that the race should either have commenced half-an-hour later on the flood, or that start should have been given to the boats which came down to the long leading. The finish of the rare was of the usual character, chiefly a drifting match down to the flag. " Maggie" teenaged to sustain the lead for some time until the captain, somewhat ill advisedly, took a long tack to windward and came right under the lea of Litton's Mill. " Maggie "consequently lost her wind and lay stationary while she was passed by " Rose" (first), " Woodcock " (second), " Gipsy " (third), and" Pink," (fourth). The judges were Messrs. T. and J. Roughley. The other officers were Messrs. E. Gleave (treasurer), Charles Naylor (secretary), and P. Truscott (number taker.)

GROCERS EVHEYWHBRE.

Zetttrs to tin COttor.

THE RECTOR OF WARRINGTON AND THE WESLEYANS.

Sir,—Your columns have recently reported an "in- struction "of the Rev. F. W. Willis upon the subject of Wesleyan Methodism. In regard to the spirit of the rev, gentleman's deliver- ance there is little or no ground for complaint. We frankly recogniso his evident desire to observe the law of cbristisn courtesy. The Rector's address neverthe- less takes, in some respects, the form of an indictment against our church. It must therefore be answered. Happily the answer will not be difficult to make. We propose to deal with the matter in the next issue of our Monthly Church Record, and in addition to this, we shall avail ourselves of the facilities which will doubtless be afforded us by the public press of the town. It is known to many of your readers that ours is a united pastorate, and I have only to add that in this communi- cation, I am writing not only in my own name, but also on behalf of my colleagues in the ministerial work of the Warrington circuit.

J. KIIITLAN, Superintendent Minister.

April sth, 1893.

TUB NEW LIBERALISM.

Sir,—Our Tory friend Mr. Arthur Bennett, is most anxious to explain away his remarks upon the occasion of Mr. A. H. Crosfield's address at the Debating Society. As a matter of fact Mr. Bennett went so far as to promise to vote for Mr. Crosfield in the event of his becoming a Parliamentary candidate for Warrington. Days after this remarkable incident, Mr. Bennett thinks it necessary to attach to his declaration certain import- ant reservations which if complied with would require Mr. Crosfield to become as big a Jingo and as noisy an Ulster Champion as Mr. Bennett. All right, Mr. Bennett ; we are too familiar with your pronounced opinions to mistake you. Whatever you may consider yourself, if you are to be judged by your own public declaration s you are • Tory of Cie deepest dye. For anyone holding your opinions to profess sympathy with the new Liberal- ism as propagated by Mr. Crosfield, reminds one of the wolf in sheep's clothing. It is very kind of Mr. Bennett to suggest tkat Mr. Crosfield disapproves of Disestablishreent and is not in favour of the Local Veto Bill. Mr. Crosfield is well able to defend his opinions upon these questions. One thing, however, is quite certain, that no candidate however able will command the support of the Liberals of War- rington who is not prepared to support a Liberal Gov- ernment in carrying both these measures.—Yours, Szc., A LIBERAL. Warrington, April 6, 1893.

&OBEY BROOK.

Sir,—You kindly granted me space in your valuable paper a year or more since, to call the attention of the public to the foul state of Sankey Brook. As far as the smell and colour is concerned it gets no better. Were I a resident householder near to the bank of that brook I should, now that the weather is fine, call a public meet- ing of the inhabitants out of doors on the brook side to protest against the continuance of so foul a nuisance so detrimental to health and so powerful an agent in dissem- inating disease. It may be said that if I don't like it, I may leave it. True, but my leaving will make it no better. If instead of complaining day after day, the householders and in- habitants generally,should hold a meeting some evening; and get up a memorial setting forth the horrible condi- tions under which they live, and the fearful stenches and «tigilsting and nauseating sights that meet their eyes and against their olfactory senses so continuously, and bring this matter before the proper authorities. They should send the memorial to the district member wbo lias promised to look after the interests of his consti- tuents. There isMr. Legh on one side and Mr. rierpoint on the other; there is also the County Council, and there should be a riparian authority who could, if they would, prevent the pollution of rivers. What are they all doing ? Time was when this brook abounded in fish. No fish could live in it now for five minutes. The only living thing I have ever seen in it was a solitary rat that escaped out of Clare and Ridgway's shipyard. There was, too, a poor demented man who crossed it one morning but died a few hours afterwards. There have been hopes held out of purificatios of the abatement of the nuisance and a return to the halcyon days of purity and clear water, and that steps should be taken to at- tain that desirable object. The steps, if taken at all, are very short and very slow. Tim time for steps has long siLce past ; it should now be strides long and rapid, to put an end to a scandalous condition of things that is a foul blot on the face of creation and a mockery of our boasted sanitary laws. Hoping something may be done speedily in the inter- ests of decency and suffering humanity. I am, sir, yours truly, Sankey Bridges, April 6,1393
