1 NOTES ON GAMES AND 1 A rinEncs.

FOOTBALL. Phew ! If there is one reason why a footballer would be a cyclist at such a time as Easter, it is because the happy wheelman has immediate access to lubrication. I don't know what this lubrication is. But the P.D. says he does ; there are two sorts, one for moistening the wheels and one for preventing the man's thtoat be- coming parched, and to prevent an unholy desire to drink out of a bucket when he gets home. That is what the P.D. says ; and he can tell you most anything from the way to play " nick-nack," to the correct manner in which you should read a Greek ode.

The Warrington first team players have had an excep- tionally brisk time of it since they commenced opera- tions with the Mona Club on Good Friday. These ex- ponents of the game from Mansland in addition to re- ceiving a licking of four goals, two tries, to a try, re- ceived a little instruction in the latter-day style of foot- ball. It was amusing to see the simple, old fashioned, antiquated methods of doing "smart things," which they came armed with. The first team met Leigh on their own territory, and in a brilliant manner one team did not beat the other. Not a bit of it. Almost the whole of the game was taken up by the forwards slush- ing around each other ; getting the ball into touch, and scrimmaging. There was a man bet ss. that Warrington would win, and he sends a note by the P.D. wanting to know if " it isn't a downright shame for the other fel- low to claim time money ; because neither of them won." So far as betting is concerned I am an unsophisticated "chile of nature." But to my way of thinking this bet is as clear as the difference between a lop-eared rabbit and a crab tree. If you bet Warrington would win and they have not won you must have lost.

===== 0002601_18930408_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
Monday saw a big, crowd of both sexes down at Wilderspool to witness the match between Rochdale St. Clements and our " bra' fast tern.';

There were two prominent features of this match. One was the contesting play of Boscow and Rigg Roch- dale St. Clements full-back whom Boscow supplanted. The great difference between these two players was that Boscow remained calm, sure, and scrupulously discreet where there was much uncertainity ;and he never failed to achieve what he intended to do. Rigg, who is cer- tainly a most excellent player, works in a different style to BOSCOW. He does not play anything like our full back's defensive game, hut in his aggressive essays he is much more daring and dashing than Boscow, but without his discretion. The other feature was the clever combined play of the two half-backs J. Bate and " Smack "of that ilk. Two of the three tries which were won were gained by the Bates. The way in which the last one was scored was amusing. There was a scrimmage a yard off Rochdales' line,two men were mark- ing J. Bate, and when he "collared "the leather he had slid under the legs of one sf his markers and before you could wink he had scored. People just howled. War- rington bad the best of the game all cound although when the Rochdale team, who are a finely built lot of men— came on to the ground fears were entertained for War- rington's welfare. -- Our good old A team beat Leigh A on Saturday by 2-1-4 to 0-1-3. Very good again!

Here is sonoe more Junior Cup competition of the War- rington brand. St. Mary's and the Rangers met on Saturday, and had a "gate" the proceeds of which was to aid the Ragged School. Nothing was scored in the first half, but in the second St. Mary's came forth beam- ing with joy and a goal,which knocked the brilliance out of the Rangers' try.

===== 0002601_18930408_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
ASSOCIATION.

Here we are again ; but please don't walk in ; the War- rington Association did ail the walking in necessary when I they met Penketh on Monday in the second round of the medal competition. The game ended in a win for my eld friends—the Warrington " blioys " by three goals to I nil. That was "walking into" them ! However, seve- ral plaintive complaints against th,- referee have reached I me from Penketh ; but somehow the referee always does ' become the scapegoat for a team of indifferent players. ' , When our Indomitable One was remonstrated with by ' big father with a stick for not asking his permission to take his tobacco, he said, "Well, yer can't please every- one, can yer ?“ The referee should say th .t. By the way, Lymm beat Warrington North End by three goals to nil, and Orford Barracks beat Orford Crescent by two goals to nil in the same competition on Saturday. That is what I call doing some "pronounced" work.

Warrington's: luck wasn't in the ascendency on Satur- day, however. They were beaten by Winnington Park by three goals to nil. But they oughtn't to lose heart. Their new players are doing very well.

BOATING. By the foregoing heading Ido not wish to infer that boating is a legitimate section of athiLtics, as this column is set aside for athletic matters. I atended the Warrington Sailing Club boat race on Saturday and should like to say something about it. I agree with the popular condemnatim which has been uttered against the fossilized style of holding a boat race, indulged in by the club. The great thing with Which I find fault is the , continuing of the race round the buoy at the Five Acres and back again to Litton's Mill. It is palpably absurd, at least on the present lines, as the result of the race on Saturday showed. Maggie the boat which won the race down from s%idnes was absolutely spoiled of its well merited prize, because luck—not qualification—turned against it in the drift on the eble from the buoy to the winning flag. I am told that the reason of the present character of the race is to supply the public with an interesting sight. Veil, the public d an't appreciate it. If it is necessary to study the public so much why not arrange a proper regatta with rowing matches and not absolutely ruin a 16 miles race for a consideration of that kind. There is no necessity to sail from Whines under the present cir- cumstances ; the race might well commence at the buoy and end opposite Litton's Mill. I hope the club will not endanger their own reputation next year by a repetition of this most unpopular and unjust way of deciding a race.

===== 0002601_18930408_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
PEN KETH.

-- THE EASTER HOLIDAYS.—This popular holiday Good Friday was entered into with as much zest as in former years. The people were early astir, and many cyclists and pleasure seekers left the village for a visit to ether parts. The old custom of holding a fair at Fiddlers Ferry has of late years relaxed. In the afternoon the annual tea meeting was held in the Wesleyan School and was well attended. Afterwards the room was cleared and a concert was given. The officers in connection with the Sunday school and chapel read the annual reports, and the prizes for good attendance were distri- buted. Mr. Taylor, police court missionary, was present and recited. At St. Mary's Mission, service was held in the evening, when the Rev. E. S. Jackson preached a very fine sermon. On Sunday morning early Communion was also held at the same place —The inhabitants as usual had a general holiday on Monday last. Many people sought pleasure in other districts, Frodsham appearing to be the favourite place of resort. Considerable interest was shown in the football match between Warrington Association and Penketh in the medal competition. Much disappointment was felt by the enthusiasts at the loss of the Penketh team, the general feeling being that there was not sufficient fair play shown towards them by the referee. PENEETH PARE.—On Friday morning last a large num- ber or persons assembled in the Penketh Park to 24sist in the raising of the gigantic flag staff. The staff is a gift to the Park. Company from Messrs. R. A. Naylor and Co.. Warrington.. .It consists of one large pole 87' feet. long and a smaller pole 20 feet long. The post is sunk 7 feet in the ground, leaving over 100 feet above the ground level. The men under the superintendence of Mr. C. E. Parker, began at 8 o'clock, the difficult task of raising the large post to the position in which it now stands. The following day the smaller pole was hoisted up and fixed. The flag was then hoisted and iseasily visiblefor many miles.

THE EXAMINEE, \TUMMY, APRIL 8. 1893.

AT OUR LOCAL CiIURCHES.

PADGATE CHURCH. Oa Sunday, Padgate Church was beautifully decorated for the services on Ester Sunday, which were very largely attended. Ihe Vicar, the Rev. J. J. Langharn, conducted the services. No less than 116 communicant' attended the sacrament, during the day.

THE P.S.A. AT WYCLIFFE. On Sunday, the Pleasant Sunday Afternoon service at Wycliffe Church, was well attended. The Rev. John Yonge was the chairman. An earnest address was delivered by Mr. W. Hickman. Mr. A. Griffiths contri- huted a solo from the " Elijah" and Mr. NV. H. Wal- lington rendered two solos. The organist was Mr. W. H. Payton. A benevolent fund from the voluntary offertories of the men has been inaugurated for the bene- fit of the members who may be in want, or when sick. New members were enrolled at the close of the meeting.

PRESENPATION OF AN ORGAN TO THE PARISH

CHURCH. A valuable three manual choral organ, presented by Mrs. William Woods, Yennell-st., to the Warrington Parish Church, was opened on Sunday. The pieces used for the opening were accompaniments to Stainer's Choral Celebration. There has been for some time much need for an organ of this kind,and Mrs. Woods,with the usual generosity of her family, has supplied the want. The tone and excellencies of the organ were very favourably commented upon, and it will, it is hoped, materially in- crease the efficiency of an already good choir.

LOCAL CONFIRMATIONS. Dr. Royston (absistant Bishop of Liverpool) held a confirmation service at St. Paul's Church, on Thursday afternoon. There was a. large attendance of parents and friends. The candidates from St. Paul's numbered thirty male, and forty-two female. There were also eight can- didates from the Bluecoat School. There were several , male and female candidates over forty years of age. 1 Before the ceremony of the laying on of hands, his Lord- ship delivered an admirable address, impressing upon the candidates the importance of the vows they were about to make. The following clergymen were also present: Rev. H. V. Pigot, Rev. E. C. E. Carleton, and the Rev. T. M. R. Young-Hughes. A confirmation was also held on Thursday at Winwick, the candidates be- longing to Winwick and Orford.

THE REV. J. YONGE AND THE RECTOR. We extract the following from the" Wycliffe Church Record "for April :—" The Pastor's aim in his rely to the Rector's criticisms was to state the case of Congre- gationalism dispassionately and clearly, and he is not without hope that he was to some extent successful in that endeavour. At any rate, it is pleasing to know that the Rector himself has recognised the spirit and tone of the Pastor's Address. In a letter received from from him by Mr. Yonge, Mr. Willis says : I write to thank you very much for the kindly, courteous, and christian tone of your remarks upon my words. I do not think that my addresses will do harm or cause had feeling. Certainly not if they are accepted in the spirit you have received them. What I shall be glad of is this, if they and the answers made, cause people to think, and care to know the truth. Anything is better than indifference, and if you hold the truth I only wish that it may prevail.' th,s acknowledgement is very gratifying and we can at any rate, sympathise with the Rector's wish that, as we believe we do hold the truth, our opinions may " prevail." We trust this general challenge of controversy may be met all round as calmly and dispassionately as we have sought to meet it our- selves. We shall have to correct the Rector's histotical allegations on the second Sunday Evening of this month, when dealing with the celebration of the Con- gregational Martyrs of 300 years ago."

to the present day. The duty of the deacons was to collect and distribute the alms of the congre- gation, to preach, if licensed by the bishap, to baptise in the absence of the priest, and to help the priest in the administration of Holy Communion. The work of the priest was to preach, and baptise, to absolve, that is, to convey God's forgivenness to those who use His ministry aright, and to administer Holy Eucharist. The bishops' work is to confirm and to or- dain. The record note was the sacraments, and the sspramental ordinances. There were two sacraments, baptism and communion which had the authority of Scripture, and the sacramental ordinances were confir- mation,holy orders, penance,absolution,hely matrimony, and holy unction—not extreme unction, which was ' Romanist. The canon of the Old and New Testaments was decided by the church in communion with the Holy Spirit. The church had a better translation of the Scrip- tures than Roma fists had, as it was translated from the originals in Greek and Hebrew, while the Romanists had a translation of the Vulgate, which was itself a translation from the Greek, and it was full of error. The church took its doctrine from the Word of God. They believed that by baptism every little infant was regene- rated. As to confirmation, only the church confirms. The Nonconformists do not pretend to confirm. Only bishops can confirm. Confirmation means strengthening the faith,by the laying on of the hands of God's minister, the bishop. Absolution was a blessed means of grace to those who used it aright. The church believed this to be optional, and not compulsory, as the Romanists be- lieve. As to Holy Communion, they did not believe in Transubstantiation, but they also did not believe in the Zwinglian idea that the communion was a mere form, but believed that after consecration there was still bread and wine as before,but that also there was an inward as well as an outward part, which was invisible, preter- naturally present. This was the doctrine of the Real Presence. He believed in making the house of God and the service as grand and as impressive as possible. It had been said that the high church were law breakers. but in his opinion the high church were the true law keepers. In conclusion, he spoke of the duty of the church among the poor, and hoped that his hearers would become more gad more attached to the church.

SANKEY BRIDGES WESLEYAN CHAPEL. SALE OF WORK AND ANNUAL TEA MEETING.

THE RECTOR CRITICISED. A sale of work was held in the Sankey Bridges Wes- leyan Schools, on Monday afternoon, the proceeds of which are to go towards wiping off the debt on the schools The articles offered for sale were remnants of the bazaar held last April in Bold-st., Wesleyan Schools.l The stall holders were Mesdames Tonge, Cotterall, High- ley, J. L. George, C. George, Pritchard, Miles,Entwistle, Biddle, W. Brough, T. Brough, Lawton, and Hardy. The sale was opened by the Rev. A. Freeman, who ex- plained that Mrs. Freeman, who should have opened the sale was, to her great regret, unable to be present, as she was unwell. He afterwards made a very humorous and interesting speech urging the claims of Sunday Schools, and showing their far reaching influence in the country.—The Rev. J. Kirtlan in proposing a vote of thanks to Mr. Freeman, explained that the schools had cost £1,200, and that there was still a debt of £270, which he hoped would be considerably reduced by that day's sale.—Mr. J. Summersgill seconded a vote of thanks which were passed very heartily. Mr. Freeman briefly replied and the sale then began. The sum rea- lised on the sale amounted to £22 15s. The annual tea meeting was held the same afternoon. Over 140 persons sat down to tea, and the ladies at the tables were Mrs. Miles, Miss Cannon, Miss Brough,Urs. Woolley, Mrs. Cotterall, Miss Coradine, Mrs. Tonge,Mrs. Hardy and Miss Morrall. After tea the usual meeting was held, the Rev. J. Kirtlaa in the chair. The Secre- tary, Mr. S. Blythe, read the report, which showed that there were 28 male and 8 female teachers ; 90 female and 123 male scholars, and 60 infants, total 273. The average attendance was, teachers, morning 9, afternoon 16 ; scholars morning, 100, afternoon 200. The officers for the year were Messrs. J. L. George and W. Brough, superintendents : Messrs. S. Blythe and F. Light, secre- taries; Mr.W.Summerfield,treasurer ; and Mr.R.Lawton, visitor. The treasurer's report showed that the income for the year was £42 4s. 5d., expenditure £42 18s. 7d., balance in hand 53. 101.—The cli,:dr afterwards rendered a glee "It was a lover and his lass," which was well appreciated. The Chairman then gave a brief address. Subsequently the Rev. A. Freeman addressed the meeting and said that he hoped to see the debt on the school wiped off before he left the circuit. He also alluded to Suuday schools,ancl showed their influence by the fact that there were six millions of Sunday school scholars in Great Britain and 600,000 teachers. He thought this was a sufficient argument in favour of Sunday school.—The Chairman afterwards referred to the recent address of the Rector of Warringten on the Wesleyans. He said that the address was a caricature en the idea of conver- sion. (Mr. Storey " Ile does not understand it.'') The Rector said that Methodists believed in sudden conver- sion. This was a misrepresentation of Methodism from the beginning. They led children from their infancy to realise the personal influence of Jesus Christ, and he (the speaker) thought that this would help them far more than the mere sprinkling of a few drops of water for regeneration. Of coorse they believed that conversion was sudden in some cases, though they also believed in leading children into enjoyment of personal religion , without those outward manifestations in the cases des- cribed.—Miss A. Morrall then sang with good effect " The last song." Mr. Sumtnersgill gave a very inter- esting account of the history of the school and Mr. F. Braithwaite of Birkenhead, formerly of Sankey Bridges also addressed the meeting. The ch7lir sang a glee entitled " The grass was weswith dew," and the meeting was closed with the usual votes of thanks proposed by Mr. Roff and seconded by Mr. Horrecks. After the meeting the remainder of the articles of the sale of work, were sold by auction by Mr. Sutnmersgill. The Easter services at e:ankey Bridges have been very successful almost of £4O having been taken.

THE RECTOR ON THE HOLY CATHOLIC CHURCH: ' - The Rector (the Rev. F. W. Willis), concluded his course of" instructions" on Sunday night with an ad: dress on the Holy Catholic Church.- He said that thia was an appropriate subject to conclude his " instruc. , Lions " with, and also an appropriate day. If what he said that night would tend to attach his hearers more closely and deeply to the church, he would be especially' thankful. He would first give a definition of . the .Holy Catholic Church, then speak upon six , motes of the church, and lastly of certain particulars which would, he hoped, attach them more closely to it. The church was a visible,but spiritual society open to all by holy bap- tism. The Greek word'for church means an assembly of a body of people,called out of the world,and a definition of it was given in Ephe5.1.,22,23. In the old testatnent times they found that there was a Jewish Church, lasting over 1,500 years how the time of Moses to Christ. He (the Rector) had fouhd the Mosaic dispensation most useful in forming an idea of the church. , He had found the Christian porresponding to the Mosaic in every point except that the Christian was more spiritual. St. Paul in one of his epistles compared circumcision and holy baptism together. Let them look at prophecy, taking for instance Ilebuchadnezzar's dream of the figure of which the head was of gold, the shouldere of silver, the body of brass and legs of iron and clay, and a stone came and dashed it in pieces. Daniel who explained the meaning of the dream showed that the different parts of the figure signified different kingdoms thus the head, the ! Babylonian, the shoulders, the Med° Persian, the body, the 'Greek, the legs of iron, the Romans. The stone which dashed the figure in pieces was the kingdom of Jesus Christ. All those were visible kingdoms and so the kingdom of Christ was alas to be visible. They would find also John the Baptist and Christ himself pro- claiming a kingdom of heaven, and in the apostolic times they'found that the admission into that kingdom was by baptism. when churches were founded in different parts, St. Paul always spoke of them as branctics of the one Holy Catholic Church. During his captivity, he formed the grand idea of the universality of the unity of the church. Thus they found in the New Testament that the churches are nos separate bodies, but are sireply branches of the one church. He would pass on to the notes Of the church. First of all, there . must be a definitely appointed ministry. In the Jewish Church there was a definitely appointed ministry of a threefold character, viz, high priest, priests and Levites. In the Christian Church during the lifetime of Christ, there was also a threefold character, viz. Christ, the aspoetles, and the disciples. After Christ ascended into r heaven, the apostles stepped into the place pf Christ, the disciples into the place of di% apostles, and a new body, . the deacons, was termed. After the apostolic age, the three orders were, the bishops or presbyters, the priests, and the deacons, which orders have continued

RUNCORN LIBERALS AND THE VETO

BILL-

The monthly meeting of Runcorn District Liberal Association was held on Wednesday night in Union Hall, when Mr. J. D., Jones read a paper on the Local Veto Bill. Mr. R. Posnett presided. Mr. Jones mentioned the fact that it was thirty years ago that Sir Wilfred Lawson introduced the Permissive Prohibitory Liquor Bill into the House of Commons for the first time. Only forty members voted for it on that occasion. The question had been brought to its present stage mainly through the efforts of the advanced temper- ance party, those whose motto is" Total abstinence for the individual and prohibition for the State," but we had now arrived at a period when all good citizens should take their share ot the responsibilitya,nd join the abstain- ers and prohibitionists in dealing with this social evil. The demands of the advanced temperance party were "direct veto. Sunday closing ; no compensation.' Ele frankly admitted that these demands were not satisfactorily met by the Goveratnent. But as a very generous, instahnent," the Bill was gratefully accepted by the temperance party' He moved that this meeting of Runcorn District Liberal Association hails with satisfaction the introduction into the. House of Commons. of the Liquor Traffic (Local Control) Bill and expresses an earnest wish that it may at an early date be placed upon the Statute Book.—Mr. J. T. Holt seconded the motion, which was supported by Mr. B. Kniveton, who said he liked the Bill because of its extreme moderation.. It permitted hotels and re- lreshment houses to exist but would put an end to the drinking shops. (Hear, hear.)—Mr. D. Bisbrown also supported the motion. He declared that this was not a teetotaller's question but a citizen's question, and he showed how it affected. not only the moral wall-being of the community but also its material prosperity.—Mr. W. Handley, did not think the country was prepared for total prohibition, and the Bill went as far as the people were prepared for.—The Chairman pointed out that a landlord now had the Bower of stopping the tale of in- toxicating liquor, and he claimed that the people should have the same power of saying whether they would have public-houses in their midst or not. It was pointed out that the hotels that did remain could only supply to travellers or lodgers and there would be no drinking bar as now. The houses would have to be bonafide hotels and refreshment houses and not mere drinking shops.— The resolution was carried un.animously, and the Secre- tary 'Mr. J. Clarke) was instructed to forward a copy to Sir William Flarcourt and Mr. J. T. Brunner, Ma'.

NARROW ESOAPE FROM DROWNING

OF A RUNCORN TRADESMAN.

Mr. Jno. Beck, son of Mr. Francis Beck, of Prod- sham (partner in the firm Messrs. Handley and Beck, iron merchants, Runcorn and Widnes), had a narrow escape from drowning on Wednesday after- noon. He was crossing Boden's Lock on the Weaver Canal, at Sutton, when a parcel he was carrying came in contact with a projection, and he was thrown into the water. The water was very low in the lock at the time, and Mr. Beck kept himself afloat by holding to a cross beam on the gate. It would have been impossible for him te clim.b up the smooth masonry of the lock, but for- tunately Mr. Leonard Pollard, of Frodsham, was in the vicinity and that gentleman obtaining a rope, successfully drew Mr. Beck from his precarious position to the banks of the canal. After a change of clothes at Mr. Boden's house, Mr. Beek was en- abled to proceed to his house at Frodsham.

UNIONIST DEIIONSTRATIOS LAST NIGHT.

Last night a "Unionist Demonstration 7 was held in the Co-operative Hall, Towns End, for the purpose of denouncing the Home Rule Bill. The two principle speakers who were announced to be present were Mr. J. Atkinson Q.C., of Dublin, and Mr. Robert Pierpoint, M.P., of Warrington. Owing to a serious illness Mr. Pierpont was unable to attend the meeting that night. Mr, Robert Davies occupied the chair, and amongst those who sup- ported him in addition to Mr. Stkinson, were Mr. F Monks, Aldermen Wright, Francomb, and Sutton. Rev. E. C. E. Carleton, and several prominent mem- bers of the Warrington Conservative Association. A resolution condemning Mr. Gladstone's Home Rule Bill was proposed by Mr. F. Monks, seconded by Alderman Sutton and supported by Mr. Atkin- son and others. The number of persons present would be about 700. As the audience. entered and left the Hall they were requested to sign a petition against the Bill.

TOWN COUN('IL 'NOTES.

Br " OVEULOOKBR."

In speaking of the Town Connell meeting on Tuesday evening, the old adage may be reversed and say that it came in like a lamb and went out like a lion. The minutes of two of the committees —the Watch and the Street Improvement—were proposed and adopted almost before it was fairly realised that business bad begun. There was, it is true, no incitement to loquacityin connection with either of them. Councillor Dixon was the first to break the " conspiracy of silence " which, it almost seemed, the members of the Council had enteted into, and he wanted to know from the Chairman of the Health Committee if a better disinfectant could not be had than that now in use. Some people, it seems, are of opinion that some of the smallpox might have been prevented if a more effective germicide had been used. Councillor Greening seemed disposed to protest against a " hot-bed " of disease being kept in the town by taking smallpox patients from the rural district. It was explained that the question of the disinfectants was about to be considered by a kind of joint sub-committee, and Alderman Sutton apologetically explained that there were only about fourteen smallpox cases from the outside district. Councillor Hill wanted to know how many smallpox cases there are in War- rington, but he was too late—the Chairman of the Committee had spoken, and the standing orders can be inexorable when it is so desired.

Councillor BEAMAN next submitted the minutes of the Sanitary Works Committee, of which he is chairman. His speech was clear, concise, and op- timistic. The new regime is working wonders at the Longford and intercepting depots—bringing order out of chaos, so to speak. There are now and have not been for the past, year any complaints of nuisance from the intercepting depots, and when arrangements have been completed,tbe possibilities of offensive odour enianating from those interesting structures will be reduced to a minimum. As to Longford depot, they had had a successful year— the " only successful year in its histOry said Mr. ' Beaman. This was mainly due to the skill and energy of the new manager, to whom high praise was given. Alderman Sutton suggested that the credit for the happy transformation of Longford might be due to Councillor Dixon. who is a com- paratively new member of the committee having charge of the depots. That gentleman, however, at once unselfishly disclaimed any special title to credit, but he added that he and one or two Friends were seeing what could be further done in the way of improvements.

The minutes of the Paving and Sewerage Com- mittee, and of the School Attendance Committee did not call for any special comment. As vice- chairman of the Museum, Scc., Committee, Coun- cillor 11. Roberts called attention to the gifts that the Committee had lately received, and he specially mentioned those which had been received from the borough member, Mr. 1L Pierpoint. The Commit- tee, it appears, are anxious to form a special gallery of Warrington worthies and Mr. Pierpeint's recent 'gifts were acceptable contributions towards this ' object. On the Cemetery Committee's minutes coming under review Councillor Muir explained how it was that some of the councillors had their funeral apparel dirtied in, the cemetery church at the burial of the late borough treasurer. A care- taker had carefully dusted every part of the chapel except one corner when he was called away, and that particular corner, as it happened, was the one in which some of the councillors found themselves.

The minutes of the Town Hall and Baths Com- mittee were moved by Alderman thandley and he explained that the new baths are to be supplied with Appleton water which is cheaper than that from Winwick. It would seem that the Appleton water has some peculiar characteristics. To those who look upon it in the baths it has a yellowish - green appearance, but this is not so much due to the intrinsic qualities of the water as to the ocular illusions caused by the " reflections of the 'roof and other muses" as Alderman Chandley put it. Coun- cillor Flood called attention to the dismissal by the Committee of the bath-keeper after sixteen years service, and Councillor H. Roberts, who also spoke against the summary dismissal of old ser- vauts,humoursly suggested that they might appoint a " consulting " bath-manager, just as they had appointed a consulting gas manager and a consult- ing head gardener.

The Gas Committee may be discribed as the "autocrat" of the Town Council Committees. ,It does not hold itself bound by the resolutions of the Council in the same way as the other committees do, and particularly it often declines to condescend to give the usual particulrs as to the tenders it In order to get some of these particulars, Councillor Roberts moved the rejection of a pro- posal to accept tenders for 1.000 tons of cannel and for a supply of gas pipes and castiu4S,' and after a rather lively debate the proposal was carried by fourteen votes to. ten. Councillor It T. Faircjouzh thereupon plaintively inquired who was going to pay for the cannel which. was • already at the gas works. Councillor 'Roberts moved another amendment on the Fina'nce Committee's minutes to the effect that ' the paying of the sum of £7,650 6s; lid., which is the cost of the smallpox epidemic up to date,should be spread over two years instead of one, and this amendment, too, was carried by fourteen votes td eleven. The concluding business of the Council was the consideration of a resolution proposed lay Councillor 11. T. Fairclough that a previous resolu- tien of the Council requiring that all tenders involving an expenditure of £2O and upwards be advertised for should be rescinded. The proposal was, however, so strongly opposed that it was withdrawn.

DEATH OF MR. WILLIAM BARLOW.

Mr. Wm. Barlow, a well known citizen of War- rington, died very suddenly, about 2.20 yesterday morning, in his .58th veer, from .failure of the heart's action. He retired to rest apparently in good health on Thursday night, but took suddenly ill during the night, and three medical men, Drs. Starkey Smith, Reginald Smith and Sherratt, were called in, but their assistance was of no avail, for he died almost immediately. The deceased gentle- man has been manager of the Liverpool and Dis- trict Bank for 19 years, and was very highly respected. In the capapity of manager of the bank, he was banker to the Corporation. He was a prominent Wehleyan, and has held offices in con- nection with the Warrington Circuit, and was at the time of his death treasurer for the foreign missions. Ile was a very popular chairman, and was frequently chosen as such at various meetings of that body. He was a widower and leaves no family. Ile had resided in Warrington since 1884. Apart from his connection with they affairs of the Wesleyan body, Mr. Barlow did not take any active part in public affairs. He was a man of cultured tastes, and his collection of pictures and other works of art is one of great value.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The fortnightly meeting was held at Dutton Workhouse, on Tuesday, under the presidency.of Mr. Ji MeLntyre. There were also present Messrs. A. R. Norman, T. Les, S. Taylor, P. Hall, 3. Knowles, John Whi tlow,. T. Lewis, T. Baxter, C.C, J. Wilkin- son, J.: Brandreth, 11, E. Heywood, E. Aaron, John Ashton (clerk), and G. Ashton (deputy elerk). THE RITE.R WEAVER NAVIGATION.

MR. BRUNNER'S PETITION. The fitst business was the consideration of the peti- tion against the Bill, promoted by the River Weaver Trustees, forwarded for the Board's support by Mr. J. T. Brunner, ALP: The petition was published in full in the proceedings 0 the last meeting. Brieflt, the petition desired the House of Lords to insert clauses in the Bill which woultlefirstly, place the navigation in the hands of a body fairly representative of the trade and the -navigation interests ; secondly, revising the tolls so as to make :them fair and equal and abolishing the exemp- tions therefrom ; thirdly, providing that' any surplus shall be applied to navigation purposes and in reduc- tion of tolls and fourthly, providing for the proper audit andmAlleation of accounts. The Clerk- presented an elaborate digest a the River Weaver Acts, and he summed up the position shortly' las folloyea-:—Certain gentlemen were empowered by an 'Act passed- in the year 1720 to make the -river Weaver navigable between Frodsham Bridge and Winsford, the reasons for the undertaking being as stated in the Act "that it would not only be very beneficial and convenient as welt for the carriage of salt and cheese, (the great manufactures and produce of the County) as 0. other goods and merchandise to and from the towns and parts adjacent, but would be a means to pre- serve the highways in the said county and be for the ' good Of the-public." I Subsequent Acts vested the undertaking in a body of trustees composed of a large number of gentlemen of Cheshire and authorised them to make and raaintain the canal from the Weaver to Weston Point. By these Acts the- trustees are authorised to charge s—For every ton of ashler stone, burr stone, and bricks, carried on the navigation. 2d. For every ton of pig metal, iron mine, ironstone,: limestone, flag, paving stonee. or millstones, 10d. For every ton of ether goods and commodities, is. Subjectehowever, as follows :—Owners and occupiers of lands adjoining the river may use pleasure boats without' paying any duty. Rock-salt carried between Winsford Bridge, and Acton Bridge Lock and used only for the purpose of being refined and made into white salt is to be charged only 61. per ton. All dung, marl, mould, soil, and bones, carried in any vessel owned or hired by any owner-or occupier of lands within fiver miles of the navigation to be used for Manuring land in any parish, township, hamlet, village, or place, through which the navigation passes and all material for any highways in the) counts, of Chester are exempt fret-neon. All corn, potatomi,:cheese, timber, and free-stone,, the produce of lands within the townships of Sutton e Clifton, and Weston, and all coals and limestone carried for the benefit of the owners and occupiers of houses or land within the same townships are exempt from toll. Shitch or sea manure is to be gotten loaded, carried and unloaded at the expense of the trustees for the use of owners and occupiers of land on the Wagon side of the canal in the township of Weston on payment of the sum of N. for every 64, square yards 0 such slutch or sea manure. 'the tells are to be applied‘as follows in repayment of the costs pf construction ; 2nd, in preserving and improving the navigation and providine certein churches and schools and the endowments of such churches ; 3rd, towards amending and repairing the public bridges in the county; 4th, towards repairing anti amending the highvsays on the county or such other public charges or uses of the county and in such manner as the justices of the county shall direct. Mr. T. Lewis did not consider it desirable that the exemptions E 0 far as regarded farmers should be abolished. Mr. ' Heywend said Mr. Brunners's proposal meant taking 10 or 15 thousand a year from the county. Mr. Aaron : And puttinc it in their own pockets. Mr. Bra telreth threteht Mr. Brunner asked rather too much. If he had only asked that- the Weaver should be mauresed by a bedy elected by ratepayers, then he would have supported tile petition. Mr. Brunnner wished to settle everything. Mr. Aaron stated that in his long acquaintance with the Weaver. as he had said on Previous occasions in that Board room, lie had seen the-Weaver trustees waste thousands of pounds. The Trust belonged to the county and he believed in making it a properly representative body. Mr. A. R. Norman said he must enter his protest against any personal reflections being cast upon- Mr. Brunner in the matter. (Flear,heare It was in a very bad taste et the last meeting, when nearly the whole of the Board admitted that they knew very little about the question to attribute Selfish motives to Mr. Brunner. As a member of the Board he entered his protest against that kind of thing. He had known Mr. Brunner as a public man for a long time, and he had never once known him to be influenced by personal matters in his public career. On the other hand he had frequently noticed that he had advocated things that he believed for the rublic gocill, that had keen detrimental to him personally. It did seem reasonable that the Weaver Trust should he a representative Board ands that was one of the main features that Mr. Brunner was trying to inausorrate. In regard toithe tolls it had been sug- gested that he was indirectly trying to influence his own pocket. tie knew for a tact that at present the Alkali trade d6erm the Weaver paid 25 per cent., or about the t, less than the Salt Union. If a revision of toile took place the Salt Union would be very likely the first to benefit ; and whatever motives Mr. Brunner might have in preparing that petition they were not selfish or personal motives. Although there were one or two points they eotild not at all endorse, they were all agreed that the Trust ought to be a representative Board. Any- one who had power to nominate a member for the Trust 'could not do so without going cap in hand and asking whether the person they intended to nominate would be agreeable to the members of the present Board. That was a state of things that reflected upon us in this age, and he thought Mr. Brunner in taking action had been ' animated by pure motives and was deserving of their support. Mr. Fleywood pretested against the construction put upon these proceedings at the last meeting by Mr. Norman. Be did not think it, was the opinion of the, Board that Mr. Brunner was acting from selfish motives, or that any member held that opinion. He did not think there was anything said at the last meeting about selfish- ness, except one member stated that it would be to Mr,

This Company has been founded to acquire, carry on, and extend two very old and well-established businesses, those of Messrs./lA.lmm, & Fx.knoLlaaving NiBYBEE BRANCHES in various centres of industry in England and Ireland (some of which have been ca ried on during the past SIXTY YEARS) In the Oyster trade, the profitable nature and exclusiveness of which trade is well knowp. The businesses are Wholesale and Retail, having two fully- licensed Public Houses and Oy ste: Bars connected therewith, and also extensive Oyster Beds at Bangor, together withlargc quentities of Oysters laid down on such beds for future con- sumption. The Bangor Oyster Beds are well known to be the best feeding groun is in this country, and have teen proved to be admirably adapted for relaying English and Foreign Oysters. The Coin , any will possess sufficient space there for planting TEN MILLION Oysters. The Company having acquired so many established whole- sale and retail branches will, by carefully watching the mar- kets and buying Oysters dire t from the grewers, and relaying them in the Company's beds until in a suitable condition for the market, very considerably extend the epei attune, and improve the retains of the purchased busi- nesses. The Vendors of these establishments are Mr. JAMES HARVEY, or Dudley, and Mr. JOHN FEARoN, of Liverpool, who have traded as leading Oyster Growers, Dealers, and Merchants for a considerable period. Both businesses are firmly established and very lucrative, and have been in their respective families for very many years. The circumstances under which these profitable going con- cerns are to be disposed of are that Mr. OUR FEARoN has decided to retire from the bushiess altogether, and Br. ' HARVEY, regarding his retirement as an exceptional oppor- tunity for amalgamating the two branches, has arranged with Mi.FAARON to acquire his separate businesses so as to be able to iransfer the combined concerns to a Company under she Limited Liability Acts, whereby, with the additional Capital thus acquired, and by opening up branches in Lon- don, Liverpool, manchester, and Birmingham fur the' whole- sale trade an i otherwise, an enormously increased trade may be developed.
